> AGENT

Deployed. Two fixes live:

1. **Task filtering** — listing tools now filter output to shared projects only for members. If Ashlyn asks "what's on the board", she'll only see Project Pyari tasks.
2. **Admin awareness** — your `chat.md` now has a MULTI-USER section so Argus knows about the roles, knows Ashlyn is a member, and knows her Vikunja user ID for task assignments.

Try it out on both sides.

> DEVELOPER

Awesome. Can we integrate the baslines and the goals into the fitness web viewer somehow? What can we do for that do you think? It would be nice to see progress towards goals somehow like a meter or something that gets ticked off when certain milestones are met. What do you thinl?

> AGENT

Cool idea. Here's how I'd think about it:

### What's automatically trackable vs manual

**Auto from Strava (already in fitness_db):**
- 5K best time — just query best effort or filter activities by distance
- 10K best time — same
- Resting HR — could trend from Strava if you wear a watch

**Needs manual logging (exercises/sets/measurements tables exist but empty):**
- Pull-ups, push-ups, plank, dead hang, squats
- Body fat %, weight
- Flexibility milestones (touch toes, etc.)

So the first question is — are you testing these regularly enough to show progress? Like if you do a max pull-up test every 2 weeks, that's data points we can plot. If it's ad-hoc, the meters would just sit there.

### Visualization ideas

A **goals dashboard page** on fit.argbox.net with:

1. **Progress bars** per goal — baseline on the left, target on the right, current value as the fill. Color-coded: red → yellow → green as you approach the target. Simple and scannable.

2. **Milestone markers** on each bar — e.g., pull-ups: 1.5 → 5 (first milestone) → 8 → 10-14 (target). Each milestone lights up when hit.

3. **Sparkline charts** underneath each goal showing the trend over time — so you can see if you're plateauing or climbing.

4. **Auto-updating for running goals** — the 5K/10K bars update automatically when you log a run on Strava. Strength goals update when you log a test via the journal bot or a future `/workout` command.

### What we'd need to build

- A `/goals` page on the fitness dashboard
- A way to log test results (could be as simple as the journal bot or a manual entry form on the web page)
- Query logic to pull best values from fitness_db for each goal

The running stuff we could wire up immediately since the data's already there. The strength stuff depends on how you want to log test results. Want to scope this out?

> DEVELOPER

But the manual logging stuff I guess you can just check my latest workouts and stuff right to see and update autoaticallly. In terms of body fat and werigh tand fleicibility yes I'll have to tell you.

> AGENT

Right, good point. The `exercises` and `sets` tables in fitness_db are there for exactly this — if you log a workout with push-ups, we can just pull the best set from your most recent session. No separate "test" needed. Your workouts *are* the data.

So the auto-trackable list is actually bigger:

**Auto from workout logs:**
- Pull-ups: max reps in a single set
- Push-ups: max reps in a single set
- Plank: longest hold
- Dead hang: longest hold
- Squats: max reps
- L-sit, ring hold, wall handstand: longest hold

**Auto from Strava:**
- 5K best time
- 10K best time

**Manual (you tell Argus):**
- Weight
- Body fat %
- Flexibility milestones (touch toes, etc.)
- Resting HR

The only prerequisite is that the `/workout` command or journal bot actually logs your calisthenics sets into the `sets` table — which is on the roadmap but not built yet. Once that's flowing, the goals page just queries the latest/best values.

We could build the goals page now with the running data and manual measurements, then it fills in automatically as the workout logging comes online. Or we could build the workout logging first and then the goals page. What order do you want to go?

> DEVELOPER

Let's discuss the workout logging first because I think that's the most important actually

> AGENT

Yeah. So right now you have the tables (`exercises`, `sets`, `measurements`) but nothing writing to them. How do you want to log workouts? A few options:

1. **Through Argus on Telegram** — you message something like "just did 3x8 pull-ups, 3x15 push-ups, 60s plank" and Argus parses it and logs to fitness_db. Natural language, no structure needed.

2. **Through the journal bot** — similar but it would need parsing logic added, and right now it's pure text logging to markdown files.

3. **A web form on fit.argbox.net** — quick entry form where you tap exercises, enter reps/weight/duration, submit.

4. **A `/workout` Telegram command** — structured flow, maybe with a template for your program.

Option 1 is probably the most natural for you — you're already chatting with Argus. You finish a set, you tell it. No context switching. The worker already has `fitness_db.py` access, it just needs tools to write to the `sets` and `exercises` tables.

The question is how structured do you want it:
- **Freeform**: "did 8 pull-ups" and Argus figures it out
- **Session-based**: you say "starting workout" and then log sets, and it groups them into a session
- **Template-based**: you have a program (Phase 1) and just fill in the numbers

Yeah that's clean. `/workout` to start a session, then everything you send is logged as sets until you `/done`. Argus doesn't have to guess whether you're chatting or logging.

So the flow would be:

```
/workout              → starts a session, Argus says "let's go" or whatever
"8 pull-ups"          → logged
"3x15 push-ups"      → logged as 3 sets
"60s plank"           → logged
"how many sets left?" → Argus can still chat, just also parses workout data
/done                 → ends session, Argus gives a summary
```

A few things to figure out:

**Parsing** — freeform means Argus needs to understand different formats: "8 pull-ups", "3x15 push-ups", "plank 60s", "dead hang 45 seconds", "10 pull-ups @ RPE 8". Do we let the LLM parse this naturally (it's good at this) or write a regex parser?

I'd say let the LLM handle it — it'll understand whatever natural way you type. The tool just needs to accept exercise name, reps/duration, sets, weight (optional), RPE (optional).

**Session state** — we need to track "is this user currently in a workout session" somewhere. Could be:
- A flag in the DB
- In-memory dict in the bot process
- A column on the messages table

In-memory is simplest — just a dict `{user_id: session_start_time}`. If the bot restarts mid-workout you lose the session, but that's fine.

**The tool** — a `log_set` tool that Argus calls when it parses workout input. Writes to the `sets` table with the session timestamp grouping sets together.

The thing is that we need a bit of fleicibily becuase for example, if I do 3 full pullups and 2 negatives in a set, how do we log that. Also how do we log like assisted pullups or whatever you know? What about half reps.

> AGENT

Good point. Calisthenics has way more nuance than just "reps x weight". You've got:

- **Variations**: assisted pull-ups, negative pull-ups, band-assisted, kipping
- **Partial reps**: half reps, negatives, eccentrics
- **Mixed sets**: "3 full + 2 negatives"
- **Holds**: plank, dead hang, L-sit (duration not reps)
- **Bodyweight modifiers**: weighted pull-ups (+10kg), band-assisted (-15kg)

I think the cleanest approach is to **not over-structure the DB** and instead lean on the LLM's parsing. Something like:

**Sets table** — keep it flexible:
- `exercise`: normalized name (e.g., "pull-up")
- `variation`: the modifier (e.g., "negative", "assisted", "band-assisted", "weighted")
- `reps`: number (nullable — holds don't have reps)
- `duration`: seconds (nullable — rep exercises don't have duration)
- `weight`: kg, positive for added weight, negative for assistance (nullable)
- `rpe`: perceived exertion (nullable)
- `notes`: freeform text for anything that doesn't fit — "3 full + 2 negatives", "last 2 were ugly"

So "3 full pull-ups and 2 negatives" could be logged as:
- Set 1: pull-up, 3 reps
- Set 2: pull-up, variation=negative, 2 reps

Or as one set with a note: pull-up, 5 reps, notes="3 full + 2 negatives"

Honestly the `notes` field is the escape hatch. You tell Argus whatever you want naturally, the LLM does its best to structure it, and anything ambiguous goes into notes. Then for goals tracking, we query the "clean" sets (full pull-ups) to measure progress, while still having the full picture.

## Summary: Fitness Database Schema & Web App Structure

### 1. CREATE TABLE Statements

#### exercises table
```sql
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS exercises (
    id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
    name TEXT UNIQUE NOT NULL,
    muscle_group TEXT,
    type TEXT DEFAULT 'strength'
);
```

#### sets table
```sql
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS sets (
    id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
    date TEXT NOT NULL,
    exercise_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES exercises(id),
    set_number INTEGER,
    weight REAL,
    reps INTEGER,
    rpe REAL,
    notes TEXT
);
```

#### measurements table
```sql
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS measurements (
    id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
    date TEXT NOT NULL,
    type TEXT NOT NULL,
    value REAL NOT NULL,
    unit TEXT DEFAULT 'kg'
);
```

These are the three strength/fitness tables. The database also has extensive Strava integration with `activities`, `splits`, `laps`, `best_efforts`, `segment_efforts`, and `activity_streams` tables for cardio tracking.

---

### 2. Existing Functions Writing to Fitness Tables

**fitness_db.py** has NO functions that write to `exercises`, `sets`, or `measurements` tables. The only write functions are:

- `store_strava_activity(activity: dict, detail: dict) -> int` — writes to Strava tables only
- `sync_strava(count: int = 10) -> str` — syncs Strava activities
- `backfill_polylines() -> str` — updates `summary_polyline` in activities
- `backfill_streams() -> str` — populates `activity_streams`

**The strength/measurements tables exist but have no population logic yet.** This is where your workout logging feature will write.

---

### 3. Flask App Structure (fitness_web/app.py)

**Database layer:**
```python
def get_db() -> sqlite3.Connection:
    conn = sqlite3.connect(str(DB_PATH))
    conn.row_factory = sqlite3.Row
    return conn
```

**Routes:**
1. `GET /` — index page
   - Queries: `SELECT id, date, name, type, distance, moving_time, average_speed, total_elevation_gain, average_heartrate, calories FROM activities ORDER BY date DESC LIMIT 50`
   - Template: `index.html` with helper functions `format_pace()`, `format_duration()`

2. `GET /activity/<int:activity_id>` — activity detail page
   - Queries all related tables: `activities`, `splits`, `best_efforts`, `laps`, `segment_efforts`, `activity_streams`
   - Parses JSON stream data and computes smoothed pace chart server-side using Savitzky-Golay filter (scipy)
   - Resamples 500 uniform distance points, interpolates altitude and lat/lng
   - Returns to template: `activity.html` with raw streams, computed chart_data, and formatting helpers

**Important:** The Flask app is **read-only on Strava cardio data**. No strength/measurements endpoints exist yet.

---

### 4. Telegram Command Registration & Dispatch

**In telegram_bot.py:**

Commands registered in `post_init()` callback:
```python
await application.bot.set_my_commands([
    ("fresh", "Clear conversation history and start fresh"),
    ("health", "Run system health check"),
    ("audit", "Review system architecture for issues"),
    ("inv", "Capture issue snapshot and create bug ticket"),
    ("roundup", "End-of-day journal processing"),
])
```

**Command handlers registered:**
- `CommandHandler("start", handle_start)` — /start
- `CommandHandler("fresh", handle_fresh)` — /fresh (calls `clear_history()`)
- `CommandHandler("health", handle_health)` — /health (admin-only, calls `run_worker()` with health task)
- `CommandHandler("audit", handle_audit)` — /audit (admin-only)
- `CommandHandler("inv", handle_inv)` — /inv <description> (admin-only, parses issue from message)
- `CommandHandler("roundup", handle_roundup)` — /roundup (admin-only, silent)
- `MessageHandler(filters.TEXT & ~filters.COMMAND, handle_message)` — all free-text messages

**Dispatch pattern:**
- Regular messages → `handle_message()` → `chat(user_msg, user_id, on_status_callback)` in agent.py
- Worker commands → `_run_worker_command()` → `run_worker(task, intent, user_id, on_status_callback)`
- All responses go through `_send_and_log()` which:
  - Logs to conversation DB
  - HTML-escapes and converts URLs to clickable links
  - Splits messages at 4096 char limit (Telegram limit)

**Admin authorization:** `_is_admin()` checks if user role in DB is "admin"; `is_authorized()` checks if user ID in `ALLOWED_USERS` config.

---

### 5. Full Tool List (tools.py)

**Document/file tools:**
- `read_file(path)` — read file, 10k char limit, resolves relative to ARGUS_HOME or VAULT_PATH
- `write_file(path, content)` — create new or overwrite file
- `edit_file(path, old_str, new_str)` — replace exact string (fails safely if not found or ambiguous)
- `search_vault(query)` — grep vault, returns top 20 files + first 3 matching lines per file
- `list_files(directory)` — list files in dir, top 50 non-hidden entries

**Fitness/Strava:**
- `get_strava(action, activity_id?, count?)` — 3 actions:
  - `"latest"` → most recent activity with full detail
  - `"recent"` → list of recent activities (default count=5)
  - `"detail"` + activity_id → specific activity

**System tools:**
- `run_healthcheck()` — infrastructure status, tool success rates, recent conversations
- `run_audit()` — full architecture audit (prompts, contracts, configs, folder structure)
- `run_investigation(issue)` — capture bug snapshot, create ticket

**Git:**
- `git_push_vault(message)` — commit and push vault changes (runs: add -A → commit → push)

**No fitness logging/editing tools exist yet** — `write_file` and `edit_file` are for vault/workspace documents only, not fitness data.

- `/log` command handler(s) in telegram_bot.py that parse user input and call fitness_db functions
- Optional: `/strength` or `/measurements` Flask routes to view logged data

## Complete Implementation Plan: Workout Logging System

### Architecture Overview

The workout logging system adds four things to Argus:

1. **Schema**: New `workouts` table + modified `sets` table in `fitness_db.py`
2. **DB functions**: Session management and exercise logging in `fitness_db.py`
3. **Tools**: `log_exercise` and `log_measurement` in `tools.py`
4. **Telegram commands**: `/workout` and `/done` in `telegram_bot.py`
5. **Prompt injection**: Active workout state flows into `build_chat_prompt()` via `context.py`

The key design decision is that the chat model (not a worker) handles workout logging directly. Workout input is short, structured, single-step -- exactly the kind of thing the chat model's direct tool-calling loop handles well. No need for `do_work` handoff.

---

### 1. Schema Changes in `fitness_db.py`

The existing `init_db()` function at line 20 uses `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS`, so we add new tables and migrate existing ones in the same function. Since the DB is currently empty (0 bytes, no tables), there is no migration risk. But we should still write migration-safe code for future-proofing.

**New `workouts` table** (add to the `executescript` block):

```sql
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS workouts (
    id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
    date TEXT NOT NULL,
    start_time TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT (datetime('now')),
    end_time TEXT,
    notes TEXT
);
```

**Modified `sets` table** (replace the existing CREATE TABLE in the `executescript` block):

```sql
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS sets (
    id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
    workout_id INTEGER REFERENCES workouts(id),
    exercise_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES exercises(id),
    set_number INTEGER,
    variation TEXT DEFAULT 'standard',
    reps INTEGER,
    duration_seconds REAL,
    weight REAL,
    rpe REAL,
    superset_group INTEGER,
    notes TEXT,
    timestamp TEXT DEFAULT (datetime('now'))
);
```

The `date` column is dropped from `sets` -- the date lives on the parent `workouts` row. The `timestamp` column captures when each set was logged.

**Migration block** (add after the executescript, alongside the existing polyline migration pattern at line 148):

```python
# Migration: add new columns to sets if upgrading from old schema
for col, default in [
    ("workout_id", None), ("variation", "'standard'"),
    ("duration_seconds", None), ("superset_group", None),
    ("timestamp", "(datetime('now'))"),
]:
    try:
        conn.execute(f"ALTER TABLE sets ADD COLUMN {col} {('DEFAULT ' + default) if default else ''}")
    except sqlite3.OperationalError:
        pass
```

This mirrors the existing migration pattern on line 148-151.

---

### 2. DB Functions in `fitness_db.py`

Add these functions after the existing `sync_strava()` function (after line 421). They form the core workout data layer.

**Session state**: A module-level dict for active workouts.

```python
# Active workout sessions: {user_id: workout_id}
_active_workouts: dict[int, int] = {}
```

**`start_workout(user_id: int) -> dict`**
- Creates a row in `workouts` with today's date and current time as `start_time`
- Stores the workout_id in `_active_workouts[user_id]`
- Returns `{"workout_id": id, "date": date}`
- If there's already an active workout for this user, returns an error dict instead of creating a new one
- On startup, recovers orphaned sessions: checks for workouts with `end_time IS NULL` and populates `_active_workouts`

**`end_workout(user_id: int) -> dict`**
- Sets `end_time` on the active workout
- Removes from `_active_workouts`
- Returns the workout summary (calls `get_workout_summary`)

**`get_active_workout(user_id: int) -> int | None`**
- Returns the active workout_id for a user, or None
- This is what tools.py checks before logging

**`log_exercise_sets(workout_id: int, exercise_name: str, sets: list[dict], variation: str = "standard", superset_group: int | None = None) -> str`**
- Looks up `exercise_name` in `exercises` table (case-insensitive). If not found, auto-creates it with `type` inferred from the data (if `duration_seconds` is present, type = 'hold'; otherwise 'strength')
- For each set in the list, inserts a row into `sets` with auto-incrementing `set_number` within this workout for this exercise
- Returns a confirmation string like "Logged 3x15 push-ups (standard)"

**`log_measurement(type: str, value: float, unit: str = "kg") -> str`**
- Inserts into `measurements` table
- Returns confirmation

**`get_workout_summary(workout_id: int) -> str`**
- Queries all sets for this workout, joined with exercises
- Groups by exercise + variation
- Returns a formatted summary like:

```
Workout 2026-05-08 (32 min)

Pull-ups (standard): 8, 7, 6
Pull-ups (negative): 3, 2
Push-ups: 3x15
Plank: 60s, 45s

Total: 5 exercises, 10 sets
```

**`get_next_superset_group(workout_id: int) -> int`**
- Returns `MAX(superset_group) + 1` for the workout, or 1 if none exist
- Used by the tool when parsing superset input

**`recover_sessions() -> None`**
- Queries `SELECT id, date FROM workouts WHERE end_time IS NULL`
- This is a best-effort recovery -- without user_id on the workouts table, we cannot map back. However, since this is a single-user system (Sumeet is the only admin), we can assume any open workout belongs to the admin user.
- Actually, we should add a `user_id` column to `workouts` for multi-user safety:

```sql
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS workouts (
    id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
    user_id INTEGER,
    date TEXT NOT NULL,
    start_time TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT (datetime('now')),
    end_time TEXT,
    notes TEXT
);
```

This lets `recover_sessions()` properly reconstruct `_active_workouts` on restart.

---

### 3. Tool Definitions in `tools.py`

Add two new tools to the `TOOLS` list (after the existing `run_investigation` tool definition, around line 143).

**`log_exercise` tool definition:**

```python
{
    "type": "function",
    "function": {
        "name": "log_exercise",
        "description": "Log exercise sets during a workout session. Requires an active workout (start with /workout). Parses the user's exercise input into structured data.",
        "parameters": {
            "type": "object",
            "properties": {
                "exercise": {
                    "type": "string",
                    "description": "Exercise name, e.g. 'pull-up', 'push-up', 'plank', 'dead hang'"
                },
                "variation": {
                    "type": "string",
                    "description": "Variation type. Defaults to 'standard'.",
                    "enum": ["standard", "negative", "assisted", "band-assisted", "weighted", "half", "eccentric", "wide", "narrow", "diamond", "archer"]
                },
                "sets": {
                    "type": "array",
                    "description": "List of sets. Each set has reps (for strength) or duration_seconds (for holds), plus optional weight and rpe.",
                    "items": {
                        "type": "object",
                        "properties": {
                            "reps": {"type": "integer", "description": "Number of reps (null for holds)"},
                            "duration_seconds": {"type": "number", "description": "Duration in seconds (for holds like plank, dead hang)"},
                            "weight": {"type": "number", "description": "Weight in kg. Positive = added weight, negative = assistance."},
                            "rpe": {"type": "number", "description": "Rate of perceived exertion (1-10)"}
                        }
                    }
                },
                "set_count": {
                    "type": "integer",
                    "description": "Shorthand: create this many identical sets (use with reps or duration_seconds at top level)"
                },
                "reps": {
                    "type": "integer",
                    "description": "Shorthand: reps per set (used with set_count)"
                },
                "duration_seconds": {
                    "type": "number",
                    "description": "Shorthand: duration per set in seconds (used with set_count, for holds)"
                },
                "superset_group": {
                    "type": "integer",
                    "description": "Group number for superset. Multiple exercises with the same group number form a superset."
                }
            },
            "required": ["exercise"]
        }
    }
}
```

The tool accepts two input modes:
- **Detailed**: `sets` array with per-set data
- **Shorthand**: `reps` + `set_count` (e.g., "3x15 push-ups" becomes `exercise="push-up", reps=15, set_count=3`)

This dual interface lets the LLM choose the simpler path for simple input and the detailed path for complex input like "8, 7, 6 pull-ups" (three sets with different reps).

**`log_measurement` tool definition:**

```python
{
    "type": "function",
    "function": {
        "name": "log_measurement",
        "description": "Log a body measurement (weight, body fat, resting heart rate, etc.)",
        "parameters": {
            "type": "object",
            "properties": {
                "type": {"type": "string", "description": "Measurement type: 'weight', 'body_fat', 'resting_hr'"},
                "value": {"type": "number", "description": "The measurement value"},
                "unit": {"type": "string", "description": "Unit of measurement (default: kg for weight, % for body_fat, bpm for resting_hr)"}
            },
            "required": ["type", "value"]
        }
    }
}
```

**Tool execution** (add to `execute_tool()` in tools.py, in the `elif` chain starting at line 172):

```python
elif name == "log_exercise":
    from .fitness_db import get_active_workout, log_exercise_sets, get_next_superset_group
    # Determine user_id -- passed through args or use None
    user_id = args.get("_user_id")  # injected by agent.py
    workout_id = get_active_workout(user_id)
    if workout_id is None:
        return "No active workout. Use /workout to start one."
    
    exercise = args["exercise"]
    variation = args.get("variation", "standard")
    superset_group = args.get("superset_group")
    
    # Build sets list from either detailed or shorthand format
    if "sets" in args:
        sets_data = args["sets"]
    else:
        reps = args.get("reps")
        duration = args.get("duration_seconds")
        set_count = args.get("set_count", 1)
        sets_data = [{"reps": reps, "duration_seconds": duration}] * set_count
    
    return log_exercise_sets(workout_id, exercise, sets_data, variation, superset_group)

elif name == "log_measurement":
    from .fitness_db import log_measurement
    return log_measurement(args["type"], args["value"], args.get("unit"))
```

**User ID injection**: The tool needs to know which user is logging. The `execute_tool` function currently doesn't receive user context. We need to thread `user_id` through. Two options:

- **Option A**: Add `user_id` parameter to `execute_tool()` and `_handle_tool_call()` in agent.py. This is the clean approach.
- **Option B**: Inject `_user_id` into tool args before calling. Hacky but zero-diff on the function signature.

**Recommended: Option A.** Modify `execute_tool(name, args)` to `execute_tool(name, args, user_id=None)`. Then update `_handle_tool_call` in agent.py to pass user_id through. The only callers are in agent.py (lines 171, 309), and both have `user_id` available in scope. The change is mechanical.

### 4. Telegram Command Handlers in `telegram_bot.py`

Add two new handlers: `handle_workout` and `handle_done`.

**`handle_workout`** (after line 233, before `post_init`):

```python
async def handle_workout(update: Update, context: ContextTypes.DEFAULT_TYPE):
    if not is_authorized(update):
        return
    user_id = update.effective_user.id
    from .fitness_db import start_workout, get_active_workout
    
    if get_active_workout(user_id):
        await _send_and_log(update, "You already have an active workout. Send exercises or /done to finish.")
        return
    
    result = start_workout(user_id)
    await _send_and_log(update, "Let's go. Log your sets.")
```

**`handle_done`** (right after handle_workout):

```python
async def handle_done(update: Update, context: ContextTypes.DEFAULT_TYPE):
    if not is_authorized(update):
        return
    user_id = update.effective_user.id
    from .fitness_db import end_workout, get_active_workout
    
    if not get_active_workout(user_id):
        await _send_and_log(update, "No active workout to finish.")
        return
    
    summary = end_workout(user_id)
    await _send_and_log(update, summary)
```

**Register the commands** -- add to `run()` (around line 254):

```python
app.add_handler(CommandHandler("workout", handle_workout))
app.add_handler(CommandHandler("done", handle_done))
```

**Update `post_init`** to register in Telegram's command menu (line 238):

```python
("workout", "Start a workout session"),
("done", "End workout and show summary"),
```

**Call `init_db` and `recover_sessions` on startup** -- add to `argus.py` (line 13):

```python
from .fitness_db import init_db as init_fitness_db, recover_sessions
init_fitness_db()
recover_sessions()
```

### 5. Chat Model Awareness of Active Workouts

The chat model needs to know a workout is active so it interprets "8 pull-ups" as exercise input rather than random conversation. There are two levels of integration:

**Level 1 (Minimal, recommended)**: Always include `log_exercise` in the tool list. The model will naturally call it when the input looks like exercise data. If no workout is active, the tool returns "No active workout. Use /workout to start one." This requires zero prompt changes.

**Level 2 (Better UX)**: Inject workout context into the system prompt so the model knows to expect exercise input. Modify `build_chat_prompt()` in `context.py` to accept an optional `workout_active` parameter:

```python
def build_chat_prompt(role: str = "admin", workout_active: bool = False) -> str:
    if role == "admin":
        chat_md = load_file(ARGUS_HOME / "prompts" / "chat.md")
    else:
        chat_md = load_file(ARGUS_HOME / "prompts" / "chat_member.md")
    parts = [chat_md, _date_context()]
    if workout_active:
        parts.append(WORKOUT_PROMPT)
    return "\n".join(parts)
```

Where `WORKOUT_PROMPT` is:

```
## ACTIVE WORKOUT SESSION

A workout session is currently active. The user is logging exercises. When they send something that looks like exercise input (reps, sets, holds, exercise names), use the log_exercise tool to log it.

Examples of exercise input and how to parse them:
- "8 pull-ups" → log_exercise(exercise="pull-up", reps=8, set_count=1)
- "3x15 push-ups" → log_exercise(exercise="push-up", reps=15, set_count=3)
- "60s plank" → log_exercise(exercise="plank", duration_seconds=60)
- "8, 7, 6 pull-ups" → log_exercise(exercise="pull-up", sets=[{reps:8}, {reps:7}, {reps:6}])
- "3 full + 2 negatives pull-ups" → two calls: log_exercise(exercise="pull-up", reps=3) then log_exercise(exercise="pull-up", variation="negative", reps=2)
- "5 weighted pull-ups +10kg" → log_exercise(exercise="pull-up", variation="weighted", sets=[{reps:5, weight:10}])
- "superset: 10 pull-ups, 15 push-ups" → log_exercise(exercise="pull-up", reps=10, superset_group=1) then log_exercise(exercise="push-up", reps=15, superset_group=1)

Acknowledge each logged set briefly: "Logged 8 pull-ups." Don't be verbose. The user is mid-workout.

If the message is clearly NOT exercise input (e.g., "what's for dinner"), respond normally — don't try to parse it as exercise data.
```

**Threading the workout state into `chat()`** in agent.py:

The `chat()` function (line 228) needs to check if there's an active workout for this user. Add an import and check:

```python
from .fitness_db import get_active_workout

def chat(user_message, user_id=None, on_status=None):
    role = _user_role(user_id)
    workout_active = get_active_workout(user_id) is not None
    system_prompt = build_chat_prompt(role=role, workout_active=workout_active)
    # ... rest unchanged
```

This is a two-line change in `chat()`.

**Recommendation**: Implement both levels. Level 1 is the safety net (tool always works). Level 2 makes the model smarter about parsing during workouts and keeps responses terse. The workout prompt could live in a file at `prompts/workout.md` following the existing pattern, or inline as a constant in `context.py`.

---

### 6. Superset Grouping

Supersets are the trickiest part because the LLM needs to coordinate group numbers across multiple tool calls within one message.

**How it works in practice:**

User sends: "superset: 10 pull-ups, 15 push-ups"

The LLM needs to call `log_exercise` twice with the same `superset_group` number. The LLM can pick any number -- but the cleanest approach is:

1. The workout prompt tells the LLM to use incrementing integers starting from 1 for superset groups
2. Or, provide a tool/mechanism to get the next superset group number

**Simpler approach**: Don't expose superset_group to the LLM at all for the initial implementation. Instead, when the LLM sees "superset:", it calls `log_exercise` twice in the same tool-calling round (the chat loop supports multiple tool calls -- line 305-309 handles `direct_calls` which is a list). The `execute_tool` function auto-assigns `superset_group` when multiple exercises are logged in the same tool-calling round.

Actually, this is overcomplicating it. The simplest approach:

- The `log_exercise` tool accepts an optional `superset_with` field (exercise name of what it's supersetting with)
- Or: just let the LLM pass `superset_group=1` for both calls. The LLM is capable of this. The prompt example shows it.

**Recommended**: Keep `superset_group` as an optional integer in the tool. The workout prompt shows the LLM how to use it. The LLM passes the same integer for exercises in the same superset. Since the chat model loop supports up to 5 rounds of tool calls (line 242), and multiple tool calls per round, the LLM can make both calls in one round.

For the summary, when displaying the workout, sets with the same `superset_group` in the same workout are shown as a superset block:

```
Superset:
  Pull-ups: 10
  Push-ups: 15
```

### 8. Edge Cases and Considerations

**Bot restart during workout**: The `_active_workouts` dict is lost. `recover_sessions()` on startup queries for workouts with `end_time IS NULL` and repopulates. This is why `user_id` on the `workouts` table matters.

**Exercise name normalization**: `log_exercise_sets()` should normalize exercise names: lowercase, strip trailing s/es for plurals (simple heuristic), replace spaces with hyphens. "Pull-ups" / "pull ups" / "pullups" should all match "pull-up". A simple normalization function:

```python
def _normalize_exercise(name: str) -> str:
    name = name.lower().strip()
    name = name.rstrip("s")  # pull-ups -> pull-up
    name = re.sub(r"[\s_]+", "-", name)  # "push up" -> "push-up"
    return name
```

**Multiple workouts in one day**: Allowed. Each `/workout` creates a new session. The summary shows the session's duration and sets, not the whole day.

**Workout without /workout**: If someone sends "8 pull-ups" without starting a workout, the tool returns the helpful error "No active workout. Use /workout to start one." The LLM relays this.

**Admin-only**: Add `log_exercise` and `log_measurement` to `ADMIN_ONLY_TOOLS` in config.py (line 36) since only Sumeet should log workouts. Ashlyn shouldn't be logging exercises through the shared bot.

**Chat model tool-call limit**: The chat model loop runs up to 5 rounds (line 242). For complex input like "3 full + 2 negatives pull-ups", the model calls `log_exercise` twice in one round. For a superset with 3 exercises, it calls 3 times in one round. 5 rounds is more than enough.

## 1. FULL FLASK APP STRUCTURE (app.py)

**Routes:**
- `GET /` — Index/Activities list page
  - Queries activities table with: `SELECT id, date, name, type, distance, moving_time, average_speed, total_elevation_gain, average_heartrate, calories FROM activities ORDER BY date DESC LIMIT 50`
  - Passes to template: `activities` (list of activity rows), `format_pace` (function), `format_duration` (function)

- `GET /activity/<int:activity_id>` — Activity detail page
  - Queries 5 separate data sets:
    - `activities` table — full activity record
    - `splits` table — kilometer splits ordered by split_number
    - `best_efforts` table — best efforts ordered by distance
    - `laps` table — lap data ordered by lap_index
    - `segment_efforts` table — segments ordered by start_index
    - `activity_streams` table — JSON stream data (distance, velocity_smooth, altitude, moving, latlng, etc.)
  - Server-side chart computation: Uses numpy + scipy.signal.savgol_filter to smooth velocity data into pace chart
  - Resamples to 500 uniform distance points and interpolates latlng for map sync
  - Passes to template: `a` (activity), `splits`, `best_efforts`, `laps`, `segments`, `streams`, `chart_data`, `raw_latlng`, `raw_dist`, formatter functions

**Helper Functions:**
- `get_db()` — Opens SQLite connection with `row_factory=sqlite3.Row`
- `format_pace(speed_ms)` — Converts m/s to min:sec/km format
- `format_duration(seconds)` — Converts seconds to h:m:s or m:s format

**Config:**
- DB path: `/Users/sumeet/matrix/web/argus/argus/data/fitness.db`
- Dev server: localhost:5050

## 2. TEMPLATE STRUCTURE

**base.html** (/Users/sumeet/matrix/web/argus/argus/fitness_web/templates/base.html)
- Simple layout: `<header>` with logo → `<main class="container">` → content block
- Loads `/static/style.css`
- Provides blocks for: `title`, `head` (for extra CSS), `content`, `scripts`
- Header has `.logo` class linking to `/`

**index.html** — Activities list
- Extends base.html
- Renders table with columns: Date, Name, Type (badge), Distance (km), Time, Pace, Elevation, HR, Calories
- Rows have `onclick="window.location='/activity/{{ a.id }}'"` and `class="clickable"` for navigation
- Activity type rendered as `<span class="badge">{{ a.type }}</span>`
- Uses Jinja2 filters: `"%.2f"|format`, `format_duration()`, `format_pace()`

**activity.html** — Detail page
- Extends base.html
- Header section: `.activity-header` with h1 and `.meta` (date + badge)
- Stats grid: `.stats-grid` containing `.stat` divs with `.stat-label` and `.stat-value`
- Conditionally loads Leaflet CSS if `a.summary_polyline` exists
- Splits section: `.splits-panel` (scrollable, max-height 400px) with table
- Map section: `.map-panel` flex container with `<div id="map"></div>`
- Chart: `.chart-container` with `<canvas id="paceChart"></canvas>` (height 200px)
- Segments table: `<table id="segmentsTable">` with rows `.segment-row` (data attributes: `data-start-idx`, `data-end-idx`)
- Best efforts table
- Laps table (conditional render if multiple laps)
- Includes 3 script blocks:
  1. Chart.js visualization (pace/elevation)
  2. Leaflet map with polyline decoding
  3. Segment highlighting interactions

## 4. COMPLETE fitness_db.py

**Database Path:** `ARGUS_HOME / "data" / "fitness.db"` (WAL mode enabled)

**Tables & Schema:**

1. **activities** (main table)
   - id, strava_id (UNIQUE), date, name, type, sport_type
   - distance, moving_time, elapsed_time, average_speed, max_speed
   - total_elevation_gain, elev_high, elev_low
   - average_heartrate, max_heartrate, average_cadence, calories, suffer_score, perceived_exertion
   - pr_count, device_name, start_latlng, end_latlng, summary_polyline, source (default 'strava')

2. **splits** (kilometer splits)
   - id, activity_id (FK), split_number, distance, moving_time, elapsed_time
   - average_speed, average_grade_adjusted_speed, elevation_difference
   - average_heartrate, pace_zone

3. **laps**
   - id, activity_id (FK), lap_index, name, distance, moving_time, elapsed_time
   - average_speed, max_speed, average_heartrate, max_heartrate
   - average_cadence, total_elevation_gain, pace_zone

4. **best_efforts**
   - id, activity_id (FK), name, distance, moving_time, elapsed_time, pr_rank

5. **segment_efforts**
   - id, activity_id (FK), strava_segment_id, name, distance, moving_time, elapsed_time
   - start_index, end_index, start_lat, start_lng, end_lat, end_lng
   - average_heartrate, pr_rank, hidden (DEFAULT 0)

6. **activity_streams** (JSON time-series data)
   - id, activity_id (FK), stream_type, data (JSON)
   - UNIQUE(activity_id, stream_type)

7. **exercises** (for strength training)
   - id, name (UNIQUE), muscle_group, type (DEFAULT 'strength')

8. **sets** (strength training sets)
   - id, date, exercise_id (FK), set_number, weight, reps, rpe, notes

9. **measurements** (body metrics)
   - id, date, type, value, unit (DEFAULT 'kg')

**Key Functions:**
- `init_db()` — Creates tables if missing, handles migration for summary_polyline column
- `get_db()` — Opens connection with row_factory, WAL mode
- `activity_exists(strava_id: int)` → bool
- `store_strava_activity(activity: dict, detail: dict)` → int (local_id)
  - Inserts activity, splits, laps, best_efforts, segment_efforts
  - Fetches activity_streams from Strava API in parallel
  - Returns local activity ID
- `sync_strava(count: int = 10)` → str
  - Fetches recent activities from Strava API
  - Stores new ones using store_strava_activity
  - Logs synced activities
- `backfill_polylines()` → str — Fetches missing summary_polylines
- `backfill_streams()` → str — Fetches missing activity streams

## Complete Implementation Plan: Workout Logging System

### 1. Schema Design

**New tables to add in `init_db()` within `fitness_db.py`:**

**`workouts` table** -- represents a workout session (one per `/workout` command invocation):

```sql
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS workouts (
    id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
    date TEXT NOT NULL,
    title TEXT,
    notes TEXT,
    status TEXT DEFAULT 'planned',   -- planned | in_progress | completed
    created_at TEXT DEFAULT (datetime('now')),
    completed_at TEXT
);
```

- `status` tracks lifecycle: Argus creates it as `planned`, user opening the page could flip it to `in_progress`, and a "finish workout" action marks it `completed`.
- `title` is AI-generated (e.g., "Upper Body Push Focus" or "Full Body Home Workout").

**`workout_exercises` table** -- the planned exercises that Argus prescribed (the template):

```sql
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS workout_exercises (
    id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
    workout_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES workouts(id),
    exercise_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES exercises(id),
    position INTEGER NOT NULL,          -- display order
    variation TEXT,                      -- standard, negative, assisted, band-assisted, weighted, half, eccentric, wide, narrow, diamond, archer
    target_sets INTEGER,
    target_reps INTEGER,                -- NULL for holds
    target_duration_seconds INTEGER,    -- for holds (plank, dead hang)
    target_rpe REAL,
    target_weight REAL,
    superset_group INTEGER,             -- NULL = no superset; same number = same superset
    notes TEXT,
    rest_seconds INTEGER                -- suggested rest between sets
);
```

- `exercise_id` references the `exercises` table (which holds canonical exercise names).
- `variation` is a free text field from a known set but stored as text to allow future flexibility.
- `target_reps` and `target_duration_seconds` are mutually exclusive: reps for strength, duration for holds.
- `superset_group` groups exercises visually and logically.

**Modified `sets` table** -- what the user actually performed:

```sql
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS sets (
    id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
    workout_exercise_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES workout_exercises(id),
    set_number INTEGER NOT NULL,
    reps INTEGER,
    duration_seconds INTEGER,
    weight REAL,
    rpe REAL,
    notes TEXT,
    logged_at TEXT DEFAULT (datetime('now'))
);
```

Key change from the existing `sets` schema: the old table has `date`, `exercise_id`, and `set_number`. The new table replaces `date` + `exercise_id` with `workout_exercise_id`, giving full traceability back through `workout_exercises` to `workouts`. The `logged_at` timestamp captures when each set was recorded.

**Migration strategy**: Since the existing tables are empty (confirmed by the empty `fitness.db` file), this is a clean start. The old `sets` table DDL in `init_db()` can simply be replaced with the new schema. However, because `init_db()` uses `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS`, to be safe, include an explicit migration/drop of the old empty table, or just rename the new table definition. Given tables are empty, the simplest approach is to just change the DDL. SQLite's `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` will preserve existing tables, so add a migration block after the `executescript` that checks if the `sets` table has the old schema and drops/recreates it if so (it's empty anyway).

**Relationship diagram:**
```
workouts (1) --> (N) workout_exercises (1) --> (N) sets
                          |
                          v
                     exercises (canonical names)
```

### 2. `fitness_db.py` Functions

Add these functions to `/Users/sumeet/matrix/web/argus/argus/src/fitness_db.py`:

**`create_workout(title, date=None, notes=None) -> int`**
- Inserts into `workouts` table, returns the workout ID.
- If `date` is None, uses today's date.

**`get_or_create_exercise(name, muscle_group=None, exercise_type='strength') -> int`**
- Looks up `exercises` by name (case-insensitive).
- If not found, inserts it.
- Returns the exercise ID.
- This is needed because Argus will reference exercises by name, and we need the FK.

**`add_planned_exercise(workout_id, exercise_name, position, variation='standard', target_sets=None, target_reps=None, target_duration_seconds=None, target_rpe=None, target_weight=None, superset_group=None, notes=None, rest_seconds=None, muscle_group=None) -> int`**
- Calls `get_or_create_exercise` to resolve the exercise name to an ID.
- Inserts into `workout_exercises`.
- Returns the `workout_exercise_id`.

**`create_workout_with_exercises(title, exercises, date=None, notes=None) -> int`**
- Convenience function that creates a workout and all its planned exercises in one call.
- `exercises` is a list of dicts, each with keys matching `add_planned_exercise` params.
- Returns the workout ID.
- This is what the Argus tool will call -- a single atomic operation.

**`get_workout(workout_id) -> dict | None`**
- Returns the full workout: workout metadata + all planned exercises + all logged sets.
- Structure:
  ```python
  {
      "id": 42,
      "date": "2026-05-08",
      "title": "Upper Body Push",
      "status": "planned",
      "notes": None,
      "exercises": [
          {
              "id": 15,  # workout_exercise_id
              "exercise_name": "Push-ups",
              "variation": "diamond",
              "target_sets": 3,
              "target_reps": 8,
              "target_duration_seconds": None,
              "target_rpe": 7.0,
              "target_weight": None,
              "superset_group": None,
              "notes": "Slow eccentric",
              "rest_seconds": 60,
              "position": 1,
              "sets": [  # actual logged sets
                  {"set_number": 1, "reps": 8, "weight": None, "rpe": 7, "duration_seconds": None, "notes": None},
                  {"set_number": 2, "reps": 7, "weight": None, "rpe": 8, "duration_seconds": None, "notes": None},
              ]
          },
          ...
      ]
  }
  ```

**`log_set(workout_exercise_id, set_number, reps=None, duration_seconds=None, weight=None, rpe=None, notes=None) -> int`**
- Inserts or updates a set for a given workout_exercise.
- Uses `INSERT OR REPLACE` keyed on `(workout_exercise_id, set_number)` -- add a UNIQUE constraint on those two columns.
- Returns the set ID.
- This is what the web API calls on each save.

**`log_sets_bulk(sets_data: list[dict]) -> int`**
- Batch version: accepts a list of `{workout_exercise_id, set_number, reps, duration_seconds, weight, rpe, notes}` dicts.
- Wraps in a single transaction.
- Returns count of sets logged.

**`add_exercise_to_workout(workout_id, exercise_name, variation='standard', target_sets=None, target_reps=None, target_duration_seconds=None, notes=None) -> int`**
- Adds a new exercise to an existing workout (for the + button).
- Auto-calculates `position` as max(position) + 1 for the workout.
- Returns the workout_exercise_id.

**`remove_exercise_from_workout(workout_exercise_id) -> bool`**
- Deletes the workout_exercise and any associated sets.
- Returns True if something was deleted.

**`update_workout_status(workout_id, status) -> None`**
- Updates the status field. If status is `completed`, also sets `completed_at`.

**`list_workouts(limit=20) -> list[dict]`**
- Returns recent workouts (id, date, title, status, exercise count).
- For the index page listing.

### 3. Flask Routes

Modify `/Users/sumeet/matrix/web/argus/argus/fitness_web/app.py` to add:

**`GET /workout/<int:workout_id>`**
- Calls `get_workout(workout_id)` from `fitness_db`.
- Renders `workout.html` template.
- Returns 404 if not found.

**`POST /workout/<int:workout_id>/log`**
- Accepts JSON body: `{"sets": [{"workout_exercise_id": 15, "set_number": 1, "reps": 8, "rpe": 7}, ...]}`.
- Calls `log_sets_bulk()`.
- Returns JSON `{"ok": true, "count": N}`.
- This is the auto-save endpoint called by JavaScript.

**`POST /workout/<int:workout_id>/exercise`**
- Accepts JSON body: `{"exercise_name": "Dips", "variation": "standard", "target_sets": 3, "target_reps": 10}`.
- Calls `add_exercise_to_workout()`.
- Returns JSON with the new exercise data (id, position, etc.) so JS can add it to the DOM.

**`DELETE /workout/<int:workout_id>/exercise/<int:exercise_id>`**
- Calls `remove_exercise_from_workout(exercise_id)`.
- Returns JSON `{"ok": true}`.

**`POST /workout/<int:workout_id>/status`**
- Accepts JSON `{"status": "completed"}`.
- Calls `update_workout_status()`.
- Returns JSON `{"ok": true}`.

**Updated `GET /`**
- Add a "Workouts" section above or alongside the existing Activities list.
- Calls `list_workouts()` and renders them in the index page.

**Import pattern**: The web app currently has its own `get_db()` that uses a relative path `Path(__file__).parent.parent / "data" / "fitness.db"`. The fitness_db functions also have their own `get_db()` using `ARGUS_HOME / "data" / "fitness.db"`. These point to the same file on the server. For the web app, rather than importing from `argus.src.fitness_db` (which would pull in config dependencies), the cleanest approach is to add the new SQL queries directly in `app.py` using its existing `get_db()` pattern, mirroring the fitness_db function logic. Alternatively, refactor `fitness_db.py` so the DB functions accept a connection parameter, and have the web app call them with its own connection. The simpler path: just add the query functions in `app.py` since the web app is a separate process and already has its own DB access pattern.

Actually, the better approach: have `app.py` import the DB path from a shared location and use the `fitness_db` functions directly. Looking at the import chain: `fitness_db.py` imports from `.config` which loads `.env` and initializes OpenAI client. The web app does not need OpenAI. So the cleanest solution is to make the web app's query functions self-contained in `app.py`, duplicating the SQL but avoiding the config dependency chain. This matches the existing pattern where `app.py` has its own `get_db()` and its own SQL queries.

### 4. Template Design: `workout.html`

Create `/Users/sumeet/matrix/web/argus/argus/fitness_web/templates/workout.html`.

Structure, extending `base.html`:

```
{% extends "base.html" %}
{% block title %}{{ workout.title }} - {{ workout.date }}{% endblock %}

{% block content %}
<div class="workout-header">
    <h1>{{ workout.title }}</h1>
    <div class="meta">{{ workout.date }} <span class="badge">{{ workout.status }}</span></div>
</div>

<!-- Exercises grouped by superset -->
{% for group in exercise_groups %}
  {% if group.is_superset %}
  <div class="superset-block">
    <div class="superset-label">Superset</div>
  {% endif %}
  
  {% for ex in group.exercises %}
  <div class="exercise-card" data-exercise-id="{{ ex.id }}">
    <div class="exercise-header">
      <div class="exercise-name">{{ ex.exercise_name }}{% if ex.variation != 'standard' %} <span class="variation-badge">{{ ex.variation }}</span>{% endif %}</div>
      <div class="exercise-target">{{ format_target(ex) }}</div>
      <button class="remove-exercise-btn" title="Remove">&times;</button>
    </div>
    {% if ex.notes %}<div class="exercise-notes">{{ ex.notes }}</div>{% endif %}
    
    <div class="sets-table">
      <div class="sets-header">
        <span class="set-col">Set</span>
        {% if ex.target_reps %}<span class="reps-col">Reps</span>{% endif %}
        {% if ex.target_duration_seconds %}<span class="dur-col">Seconds</span>{% endif %}
        {% if ex.target_weight %}<span class="weight-col">Weight</span>{% endif %}
        <span class="rpe-col">RPE</span>
      </div>
      {% for s in range(1, (ex.target_sets or 3) + 1) %}
      <div class="set-row" data-set-number="{{ s }}">
        <span class="set-col set-number">{{ s }}</span>
        {% if ex.target_reps %}
        <input type="number" class="reps-input" inputmode="numeric" pattern="[0-9]*" 
               placeholder="{{ ex.target_reps }}" value="{{ ex.sets[s-1].reps if ex.sets|length >= s else '' }}">
        {% endif %}
        {% if ex.target_duration_seconds %}
        <input type="number" class="dur-input" inputmode="numeric" pattern="[0-9]*"
               placeholder="{{ ex.target_duration_seconds }}" value="{{ ex.sets[s-1].duration_seconds if ex.sets|length >= s else '' }}">
        {% endif %}
        {% if ex.target_weight %}
        <input type="number" class="weight-input" inputmode="decimal"
               placeholder="{{ ex.target_weight }}" value="{{ ex.sets[s-1].weight if ex.sets|length >= s else '' }}">
        {% endif %}
        <input type="number" class="rpe-input" inputmode="decimal" step="0.5" min="1" max="10"
               placeholder="{{ ex.target_rpe or '' }}" value="{{ ex.sets[s-1].rpe if ex.sets|length >= s else '' }}">
      </div>
      {% endfor %}
    </div>
    
    <textarea class="exercise-comment" placeholder="Notes...">{{ ex.logged_notes or '' }}</textarea>
  </div>
  {% endfor %}
  
  {% if group.is_superset %}
  </div>
  {% endif %}
{% endfor %}

<!-- Add exercise button -->
<button class="add-exercise-btn" id="addExerciseBtn">+ Add Exercise</button>

<!-- Add exercise modal (hidden by default) -->
<div class="modal" id="addExerciseModal" style="display:none">
  <div class="modal-content">
    <input type="text" id="newExerciseName" placeholder="Exercise name">
    <select id="newExerciseVariation">
      <option value="standard">Standard</option>
      <option value="negative">Negative</option>
      <option value="assisted">Assisted</option>
      <!-- etc -->
    </select>
    <input type="number" id="newExerciseSets" placeholder="Sets" value="3">
    <input type="number" id="newExerciseReps" placeholder="Reps">
    <input type="number" id="newExerciseDuration" placeholder="Duration (s)">
    <button id="confirmAddExercise">Add</button>
    <button id="cancelAddExercise">Cancel</button>
  </div>
</div>

<!-- Save + finish -->
<div class="workout-actions">
  <button class="save-btn" id="saveBtn">Save Progress</button>
  <button class="finish-btn" id="finishBtn">Finish Workout</button>
</div>
<div class="save-status" id="saveStatus"></div>
{% endblock %}

{% block scripts %}
<script>
// ... JavaScript for save, add/remove exercise, auto-save
</script>
{% endblock %}
```

**Mobile UX decisions:**

1. **Input fields**: Use `inputmode="numeric"` for reps/duration and `inputmode="decimal"` for weight/RPE. This brings up the number pad on mobile. The `pattern="[0-9]*"` attribute further helps on iOS.

2. **Placeholders as targets**: The `placeholder` on each input shows what the AI prescribed (e.g., `placeholder="8"` for 8 reps). The user types what they actually did. Empty means they haven't logged that set yet.

3. **Target display**: Above the sets table, show the target in human-readable form using a `format_target()` helper:
   - Reps exercise: "3 x 8 @ RPE 7" or "3 x 8"
   - Hold exercise: "3 x 60s hold"
   - Weighted: "3 x 8 @ 20kg RPE 7"

4. **Auto-save with manual save button**: Debounced auto-save on every input change (500ms debounce). Also a visible "Save Progress" button for confidence. The save status text shows "Saved" / "Saving..." / "Unsaved changes". This dual approach gives the convenience of auto-save with the reassurance of a manual button.

5. **Big tap targets**: The exercise cards have comfortable padding. Inputs are at least 44px tall (iOS minimum tap target). The +/x buttons are large enough for touch.

6. **Superset grouping**: Exercises with the same `superset_group` are wrapped in a `superset-block` div with a left border accent and a "Superset" label, visually grouping them.

7. **Add exercise flow**: The + button opens a simple modal/sheet at the bottom of the page. Just exercise name, variation dropdown, sets, reps (or duration). Submit via AJAX, and the new exercise card appears in the DOM without a page reload.

8. **Remove exercise**: The x button on each exercise header sends a DELETE request and removes the card from the DOM with a brief confirmation.

### 6. JavaScript (inline in workout.html)

The JS handles three concerns: save, add exercise, remove exercise.

**Auto-save with debounce:**
```javascript
let saveTimer = null;
const SAVE_DELAY = 800; // ms

function collectAllSets() {
    const sets = [];
    document.querySelectorAll('.exercise-card').forEach(card => {
        const exerciseId = parseInt(card.dataset.exerciseId);
        card.querySelectorAll('.set-row').forEach(row => {
            const setNumber = parseInt(row.dataset.setNumber);
            const reps = row.querySelector('.reps-input')?.value || null;
            const duration = row.querySelector('.dur-input')?.value || null;
            const weight = row.querySelector('.weight-input')?.value || null;
            const rpe = row.querySelector('.rpe-input')?.value || null;
            // Only include if at least one field has data
            if (reps || duration || weight || rpe) {
                sets.push({
                    workout_exercise_id: exerciseId,
                    set_number: setNumber,
                    reps: reps ? parseInt(reps) : null,
                    duration_seconds: duration ? parseInt(duration) : null,
                    weight: weight ? parseFloat(weight) : null,
                    rpe: rpe ? parseFloat(rpe) : null,
                });
            }
        });
    });
    return sets;
}

function save() {
    const sets = collectAllSets();
    const status = document.getElementById('saveStatus');
    status.textContent = 'Saving...';
    fetch(`/workout/WORKOUT_ID/log`, {
        method: 'POST',
        headers: {'Content-Type': 'application/json'},
        body: JSON.stringify({sets: sets}),
    })
    .then(r => r.json())
    .then(d => { status.textContent = d.ok ? 'Saved' : 'Error saving'; })
    .catch(() => { status.textContent = 'Failed to save'; });
}

function scheduleSave() {
    clearTimeout(saveTimer);
    document.getElementById('saveStatus').textContent = 'Unsaved changes...';
    saveTimer = setTimeout(save, SAVE_DELAY);
}

// Attach to all inputs
document.querySelectorAll('.exercise-card input, .exercise-card textarea').forEach(el => {
    el.addEventListener('input', scheduleSave);
});

document.getElementById('saveBtn').addEventListener('click', save);
```

```javascript

document.getElementById('cancelAddExercise').addEventListener('click', () => {

        target_sets: parseInt(document.getElementById('newExerciseSets').value) || 3,

    };
    fetch(`/workout/WORKOUT_ID/exercise`, {

    .then(ex => {
        // Build new exercise card DOM and append before the add button
        // ... (render exercise card from response data)
        document.getElementById('addExerciseModal').style.display = 'none';
    });
});
```

**Remove exercise:**
```javascript
document.querySelectorAll('.remove-exercise-btn').forEach(btn => {
    btn.addEventListener('click', function() {
        const card = this.closest('.exercise-card');
        const exerciseId = card.dataset.exerciseId;
        if (confirm('Remove this exercise?')) {
            fetch(`/workout/WORKOUT_ID/exercise/${exerciseId}`, {method: 'DELETE'})
            .then(r => r.json())
            .then(() => card.remove());
        }
    });
});
```

**Finish workout:**
```javascript
document.getElementById('finishBtn').addEventListener('click', () => {
    save(); // save any pending data first
    setTimeout(() => {
        fetch(`/workout/WORKOUT_ID/status`, {
            method: 'POST',
            headers: {'Content-Type': 'application/json'},
            body: JSON.stringify({status: 'completed'}),
        })
        .then(r => r.json())
        .then(() => {
            document.querySelector('.badge').textContent = 'completed';
            document.getElementById('finishBtn').disabled = true;
            document.getElementById('finishBtn').textContent = 'Done';
        });
    }, 1000); // wait for save to complete
});
```

### 8. Telegram `/workout` Command

Add to `/Users/sumeet/matrix/web/argus/argus/src/telegram_bot.py`:

```python
async def handle_workout(update: Update, context: ContextTypes.DEFAULT_TYPE):
    if not is_authorized(update):
        return
    user_msg = update.message.text.replace("/workout", "").strip()
    if not user_msg:
        user_msg = "I want to do a workout. Ask me what I have available and what I want to focus on, then create a plan."
    
    # This goes through the normal chat flow, which has access to create_workout tool
    user_id = update.effective_user.id
    log.info(f"Workout command from {update.effective_user.username}: {user_msg[:100]}")
    
    loop = asyncio.get_event_loop()
    done = asyncio.Event()
    
    async def keep_typing():
        while not done.is_set():
            try:
                await update.message.chat.send_action("typing")
            except Exception:
                pass
            try:
                await asyncio.wait_for(done.wait(), timeout=4)
                break
            except asyncio.TimeoutError:
                pass
    
    typing_task = asyncio.create_task(keep_typing())
    
    def on_status(text: str):
        try:
            future = asyncio.run_coroutine_threadsafe(
                _send_and_log(update, text), loop)
            future.result(timeout=10)
        except Exception:
            pass
    
    try:
        reply = await loop.run_in_executor(
            None, lambda: chat(user_msg, user_id=user_id, on_status=on_status))
    except Exception as e:
        reply = f"Something went wrong: {e}"
    finally:
        done.set()
        await typing_task
    
    safe = _html_safe(reply)
    for i in range(0, len(safe), 4096):
        await update.message.reply_text(safe[i : i + 4096], parse_mode="HTML")
```

Register it in `run()`:
```python
app.add_handler(CommandHandler("workout", handle_workout))
```

Add to `post_init()`:
```python
("workout", "Plan and start a workout session"),
```

**The flow**: `/workout` is actually just a regular chat message that goes through the normal `chat()` function. The AI has the `create_workout` tool available. The user can have a back-and-forth conversation:

1. User: `/workout`
2. Argus: "What are you working with today? Home or gym? Pull-up bar?"
3. User: "Home, no bar, want to focus on push and core"
4. Argus calls `create_workout` tool with a plan
5. Argus: "Here's your workout: https://fit.argbox.net/workout/42"

The key insight: `/workout` does NOT need to be a worker command. It just enters the normal chat flow. The chat model can call `create_workout` directly (it's a single tool call, not multi-step). If the user wants a more elaborate plan, the chat model can hand off to `do_work` for research into history, but typically this is a direct tool call.

Alternatively, if we want a richer planning experience where the worker model reads workout history, past performance, and the training program, then `/workout` should use `_run_worker_command`. But the simpler path is to make `create_workout` a direct chat tool and let the chat model handle the conversation naturally. The user can provide all context in the chat.

**Recommendation**: Keep it as a normal chat flow (not a worker command). The `/workout` command just sets context by injecting a system-level hint. If the user sends `/workout I'm at home, no bar, push day`, it feeds that through `chat()`. The chat model has `create_workout` as a direct tool. For more complex planning that needs workout history, the chat model can use `do_work`.

### 11. Implementation Sequence

2. Add migration logic for the old empty sets table
3. Implement all DB functions: `create_workout_with_exercises`, `get_workout`, `log_set`, `log_sets_bulk`, `add_exercise_to_workout`, `remove_exercise_from_workout`, `update_workout_status`, `list_workouts`, `get_or_create_exercise`

**Phase 2: Web app** (app.py + templates + CSS)
1. Add Flask routes: GET /workout/<id>, POST /workout/<id>/log, POST /workout/<id>/exercise, DELETE /workout/<id>/exercise/<id>, POST /workout/<id>/status
2. Create workout.html template
3. Add CSS to style.css
4. Update index.html with workouts section
5. Add a `format_target()` helper function in app.py

**Phase 3: Argus integration** (tools.py + telegram_bot.py + chat.md)
1. Add `create_workout` tool definition to TOOLS list
2. Add execution branch in `execute_tool()`
3. Add `/workout` command handler in telegram_bot.py
4. Register command in `run()` and `post_init()`
5. Update chat.md prompt with workout tool guidance
6. Update references/telegram.md with new command

### 12. Potential Challenges and Mitigations

**Challenge 1: The web app and Argus bot share the same SQLite file.** SQLite with WAL mode (already configured in `get_db()`) handles concurrent readers well. The web app reads mostly and writes only when the user logs sets. The bot writes only when creating a workout. Concurrent write conflicts are unlikely given single-user usage, but WAL mode handles this gracefully.

**Challenge 2: The Flask app uses a relative DB path while fitness_db.py uses ARGUS_HOME.** On the server, `Path(__file__).parent.parent / "data"` from app.py and `ARGUS_HOME / "data"` from fitness_db.py both resolve to `/root/argus-agents/argus/data/fitness.db`. But for the web app's workout routes, write the query functions directly in app.py using its own `get_db()` to avoid importing the full config chain. This is consistent with the existing pattern.

**Challenge 3: Auto-save reliability on flaky mobile connections.** The debounced auto-save plus manual save button covers this. The UI shows save status clearly. If a save fails, the data remains in the input fields and the user can retry. Consider adding localStorage backup as a future enhancement.

**Challenge 4: Exercise name normalization.** The `get_or_create_exercise` function should normalize exercise names (lowercase, strip whitespace) to avoid duplicates like "Push-ups" vs "push-ups" vs "Push ups". Use `COLLATE NOCASE` on the exercises table or normalize in the function.

**Challenge 5: Adding exercises from the web UI needs exercise name suggestions.** For now, use a free-text input. A future enhancement could add autocomplete from the exercises table. Keep it simple for v1.

### Critical Files for Implementation
- `/Users/sumeet/matrix/web/argus/argus/src/fitness_db.py` -- schema changes and all new DB functions
- `/Users/sumeet/matrix/web/argus/argus/fitness_web/app.py` -- new Flask routes for workout CRUD and logging API
- `/Users/sumeet/matrix/web/argus/argus/src/tools.py` -- new `create_workout` tool definition and execution
- `/Users/sumeet/matrix/web/argus/argus/src/telegram_bot.py` -- `/workout` command handler and registration
- `/Users/sumeet/matrix/web/argus/argus/fitness_web/static/style.css` -- mobile-first workout card styles
```

1	"""Fitness dashboard — read-only web viewer for workout data."""
2	
3	import json
4	import sqlite3
5	from pathlib import Path
6	
7	import numpy as np
8	from scipy.signal import savgol_filter
9	from flask import Flask, render_template
10	
11	DB_PATH = Path(__file__).parent.parent / "data" / "fitness.db"
12	
13	app = Flask(__name__)
14	
15	
16	def get_db() -> sqlite3.Connection:
17	    conn = sqlite3.connect(str(DB_PATH))
18	    conn.row_factory = sqlite3.Row
19	    return conn
20	
21	
22	def format_pace(speed_ms):
23	    if not speed_ms or speed_ms <= 0:
24	        return "N/A"
25	    pace_s = 1000 / speed_ms
26	    return f"{int(pace_s // 60)}:{int(pace_s % 60):02d}/km"
27	
28	
29	def format_duration(seconds):
30	    if not seconds:
31	        return "N/A"
32	    seconds = int(seconds)
33	    h, m, s = seconds // 3600, (seconds % 3600) // 60, seconds % 60
34	    if h > 0:
35	        return f"{h}:{m:02d}:{s:02d}"
36	    return f"{m}:{s:02d}"
37	
38	
39	@app.route("/")
40	def index():
41	    conn = get_db()
42	    activities = conn.execute("""
43	        SELECT id, date, name, type, distance, moving_time,
44	               average_speed, total_elevation_gain, average_heartrate, calories
45	        FROM activities ORDER BY date DESC LIMIT 50
46	    """).fetchall()
47	    conn.close()
48	    return render_template("index.html", activities=activities,
49	                           format_pace=format_pace, format_duration=format_duration)
50	
51	
52	@app.route("/activity/<int:activity_id>")
53	def activity_detail(activity_id):
54	    conn = get_db()
55	    activity = conn.execute("SELECT * FROM activities WHERE id = ?", (activity_id,)).fetchone()
56	    if not activity:
57	        return "Activity not found", 404
58	    splits = conn.execute(
59	        "SELECT * FROM splits WHERE activity_id = ? ORDER BY split_number",
60	        (activity_id,)
61	    ).fetchall()
62	    best_efforts = conn.execute(
63	        "SELECT * FROM best_efforts WHERE activity_id = ? ORDER BY distance",
64	        (activity_id,)
65	    ).fetchall()
66	    laps = conn.execute(
67	        "SELECT * FROM laps WHERE activity_id = ? ORDER BY lap_index",
68	        (activity_id,)
69	    ).fetchall()
70	    segments = conn.execute(
71	        "SELECT * FROM segment_efforts WHERE activity_id = ? ORDER BY start_index",
72	        (activity_id,)
73	    ).fetchall()
74	    streams = {}
75	    for row in conn.execute("SELECT stream_type, data FROM activity_streams WHERE activity_id = ?", (activity_id,)):
76	        streams[row["stream_type"]] = json.loads(row["data"])
77	    conn.close()
78	
79	    # Compute smoothed pace chart data server-side using Savitzky-Golay filter
80	    chart_data = None
81	    if "distance" in streams and "velocity_smooth" in streams:
82	        rawDist = np.array(streams["distance"])
83	        rawVel = np.array(streams["velocity_smooth"])
84	        rawAlt = np.array(streams.get("altitude", []))
85	        rawMoving = streams.get("moving", [])
86	
87	        # Filter moving=false and duplicate distances
88	        mask = np.ones(len(rawDist), dtype=bool)
89	        for i in range(len(rawDist)):
90	            if rawMoving and rawMoving[i] == False:
91	                mask[i] = False
92	            if i > 0 and rawDist[i] == rawDist[i - 1]:
93	                mask[i] = False
94	        fDist, fVel = rawDist[mask], rawVel[mask]
95	        fAlt = rawAlt[mask] if len(rawAlt) else np.array([])
96	
97	        # Resample to 500 uniform distance points
98	        N = 500
99	        uDist = np.linspace(0, fDist[-1], N)
100	        rVel = np.interp(uDist, fDist, fVel)
101	        rAlt = np.interp(uDist, fDist, fAlt).tolist() if len(fAlt) else []
102	
103	        # Resample latlng for map sync
104	        rawLatlng = streams.get("latlng", [])
105	        rLatlng = []
106	        if rawLatlng:
107	            fLat = np.array([ll[0] for ll in rawLatlng])[mask]
108	            fLng = np.array([ll[1] for ll in rawLatlng])[mask]
109	            rLat = np.interp(uDist, fDist, fLat)
110	            rLng = np.interp(uDist, fDist, fLng)
111	            rLatlng = [[round(float(rLat[i]), 6), round(float(rLng[i]), 6)] for i in range(N)]
112	
113	        # Convert to pace, apply Savitzky-Golay
114	        rPace = np.where(rVel > 0.3, 1000.0 / rVel, 700.0)
115	        rPace = np.minimum(rPace, 700.0)
116	        smoothPace = savgol_filter(rPace, window_length=21, polyorder=3)
117	        smoothPace = np.clip(smoothPace, 150, 700)
118	
119	        chart_data = {
120	            "dist": (uDist / 1000).tolist(),
121	            "pace": smoothPace.tolist(),
122	            "alt": rAlt,
123	            "latlng": rLatlng,
124	        }
125	
126	    # Raw latlng for segment highlighting
127	    raw_latlng = streams.get("latlng", [])
128	    raw_dist = streams.get("distance", [])
129	
130	    return render_template("activity.html", a=activity, splits=splits,
131	                           best_efforts=best_efforts, laps=laps, streams=streams,
132	                           segments=segments, chart_data=chart_data,
133	                           raw_latlng=raw_latlng, raw_dist=raw_dist,
134	                           format_pace=format_pace, format_duration=format_duration)
135	
136	
137	if __name__ == "__main__":
138	    app.run(host="127.0.0.1", port=5050)
139	
```

1	{% extends "base.html" %}
2	{% block title %}Activities{% endblock %}
3	
4	{% block content %}
5	<h1>Activities</h1>
6	
7	{% if activities %}
8	<table>
9	    <thead>
10	        <tr>
11	            <th>Date</th>
12	            <th>Name</th>
13	            <th>Type</th>
14	            <th>Distance</th>
15	            <th>Time</th>
16	            <th>Pace</th>
17	            <th>Elevation</th>
18	            <th>HR</th>
19	            <th>Calories</th>
20	        </tr>
21	    </thead>
22	    <tbody>
23	        {% for a in activities %}
24	        <tr onclick="window.location='/activity/{{ a.id }}'" class="clickable">
25	            <td>{{ a.date }}</td>
26	            <td>{{ a.name }}</td>
27	            <td><span class="badge">{{ a.type }}</span></td>
28	            <td>{{ "%.2f"|format(a.distance / 1000) }} km</td>
29	            <td>{{ format_duration(a.moving_time) }}</td>
30	            <td>{{ format_pace(a.average_speed) }}</td>
31	            <td>{{ "%.0f"|format(a.total_elevation_gain or 0) }}m</td>
32	            <td>{{ "%.0f"|format(a.average_heartrate) if a.average_heartrate else "-" }}</td>
33	            <td>{{ "%.0f"|format(a.calories) if a.calories else "-" }}</td>
34	        </tr>
35	        {% endfor %}
36	    </tbody>
37	</table>
38	{% else %}
39	<p>No activities yet. Sync from Strava to get started.</p>
40	{% endif %}
41	{% endblock %}
42

Change signature from `execute_tool(name, args)` to `execute_tool(name, args, user_id=None)`.\n\n**Add execution branches** in `execute_tool()`:\n- `log_exercise`: check active workout, parse shorthand vs detailed format, call `log_exercise_sets()`\n- `log_measurement`: call `log_measurement()`\n\n### 3. `argus/src/agent.py` — Thread user_id to tools + workout context\n\n**Pass `user_id` through to `execute_tool()`:**\n- `_handle_tool_call(name, args, role, user_id)` — adds user_id param\n- Both callers in `chat()` (direct_calls loop) and `run_worker()` (tool loop) pass user_id\n\n**Check workout state in `chat()`:**\n```python\nfrom .fitness_db import get_active_workout\nworkout_active = get_active_workout(user_id) is not None\nsystem_prompt = build_chat_prompt(role=role, workout_active=workout_active)\n```\n\n### 4. `argus/src/context.py` — Workout prompt injection\n\n**Add `workout_active` param to `build_chat_prompt()`:**\n```python\ndef build_chat_prompt(role=\"admin\", workout_active=False):\n    ...\n    if workout_active:\n        parts.append(load_file(ARGUS_HOME / \"prompts\" / \"workout.md\"))\n    ...\n```\n\n### 5. `argus/prompts/workout.md` — New file: workout parsing prompt\n\nTells the LLM:\n- A workout session is active, parse exercise input\n- Examples of freeform → tool call mapping (reps, holds, sets, supersets, variations)\n- Keep responses terse during workout (user is mid-session)\n- Don't force-parse non-exercise messages\n\n### 6. `argus/src/telegram_bot.py` — /workout and /done commands\n\n**Add `handle_workout()`:**\n- Calls `fitness_db.start_workout(user_id)`\n- Replies \"Let's go.
