  "new_string": "# Workout Logging System\n\n## Context\n\nBuilding a workout system where Argus (Telegram) generates workout plans and fit.argbox.net is the workout card for logging. The flow:\n\n1. `/workout` on Telegram → chat with Argus about equipment, focus, etc.\n2. Argus generates a plan, saves to DB, sends link: `fit.argbox.net/workout/42`\n3. User taps link → mobile-first workout card with exercises, targets, input boxes\n4. User fills in actual reps/duration between sets, adds notes\n5. Can add/remove exercises with +/x buttons\n6. Auto-saves + manual save button\n7. \"Finish Workout\" marks session complete\n\nNo chat on website. All AI stays in Argus. The web page is just a form/tracker.\n\n## Schema Design\n\n### New tables (add to `init_db()` in fitness_db.py)\n\n**`workouts`** — session container:\n```sql\nCREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS workouts (\n    id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,\n    date TEXT NOT NULL,\n    title TEXT,\n    notes TEXT,\n    status TEXT DEFAULT 'planned',\n    created_at TEXT DEFAULT (datetime('now')),\n    completed_at TEXT\n);\n```\n\n**`workout_exercises`** — planned exercises (the AI-generated template):\n```sql\nCREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS workout_exercises (\n    id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,\n    workout_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES workouts(id),\n    exercise_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES exercises(id),\n    position INTEGER NOT NULL,\n    variation TEXT DEFAULT 'standard',\n    target_sets INTEGER,\n    target_reps INTEGER,\n    target_duration_seconds INTEGER,\n    target_rpe REAL,\n    target_weight REAL,\n    superset_group INTEGER,\n    notes TEXT,\n    rest_seconds INTEGER\n);\n```\n\n**Replace `sets` table** — actual performed sets (linked to planned exercises):\n```sql\nCREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS sets (\n    id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,\n    workout_exercise_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES workout_exercises(id),\n    set_number INTEGER NOT NULL,\n    reps INTEGER,\n    duration_seconds INTEGER,\n    weight REAL,\n    rpe REAL,\n    notes TEXT,\n    logged_at TEXT DEFAULT (datetime('now')),\n    UNIQUE(workout_exercise_id, set_number)\n);\n```\n\n**Relationship:** `workouts` 1→N `workout_exercises` 1→N `sets`, with `exercises` as the canonical name catalog.\n\n### Migration\nThe existing `sets` table is empty (fitness.db is unused), so we can safely drop and recreate. Add migration block after `executescript` that checks schema and handles it.\n\n## Changes by File\n\n### 1. `argus/src/fitness_db.py` — Schema + DB functions\n\n**Schema:** Add `workouts` and `workout_exercises` tables to `init_db()`. Replace `sets` DDL. Add migration for existing DBs.\n\n**New functions:**\n- `get_or_create_exercise(name, muscle_group, type)` → exercise_id. Normalizes name (lowercase, strip plurals, hyphens).\n- `create_workout_with_exercises(title, exercises_list, date, notes)` → workout_id. Atomic: creates workout + all planned exercises. This is what the Argus tool calls.\n- `get_workout(workout_id)` → dict with full workout detail (exercises + logged sets).\n- `log_set(workout_exercise_id, set_number, reps, duration_seconds, weight, rpe, notes)` → set_id. Uses INSERT OR REPLACE on UNIQUE(workout_exercise_id, set_number).\n- `log_sets_bulk(sets_data)` → count. Batch version in single transaction.\n- `add_exercise_to_workout(workout_id, exercise_name, variation, target_sets, target_reps, target_duration_seconds, notes)` → workout_exercise_id. Auto-calculates position.\n- `remove_exercise_from_workout(workout_exercise_id)` → bool. Deletes exercise + its sets.\n- `update_workout_status(workout_id, status)` — sets completed_at if status=completed.\n- `list_workouts(limit)` → list of recent workouts for index page.\n- `log_measurement(type, value, unit)` → str. Inserts into measurements table.\n\n### 2. `argus/fitness_web/app.py` — Flask routes\n\n**New routes (all use app.py's own `get_db()` to avoid importing config chain):**\n- `GET /workout/<id>` — renders `workout.html` with full workout detail\n- `POST /workout/<id>/log` — JSON API, accepts `{sets: [...]}`, calls log_sets_bulk. Auto-save endpoint.\n- `POST /workout/<id>/exercise` — JSON API, adds exercise to workout, returns new exercise data\n- `DELETE /workout/<id>/exercise/<eid>` — JSON API, removes exercise + its sets\n- `POST /workout/<id>/status` — JSON API, marks workout completed\n\n**Update `GET /`** — add workouts section above activities list.\n\n**New helper:** `format_target(ex)` — returns human-readable target like \"3 x 8 @ RPE 7\" or \"3 x 60s hold\".\n\n### 3. `argus/fitness_web/templates/workout.html` — Mobile-first workout card\n\n- Extends `base.html`\n- Header: title + date + status badge\n- Exercise cards: name, variation badge, target display, set input rows, notes textarea, x button\n- Each set row: set number + input fields (reps or duration, weight if applicable, RPE). Placeholders show targets.\n- Superset grouping: exercises with same `superset_group` wrapped in `.superset-block` with orange left border\n- `+ Add Exercise` button → bottom sheet modal (exercise name, variation dropdown, sets, reps/duration)\n- Save Progress button + Finish Workout button\n- Save status indicator (\"Saved\" / \"Saving...\" / \"Unsaved changes\")\n- Inputs: `inputmode=\"numeric\"` for number pad on mobile, min-height 44px for tap targets\n\n**JavaScript (inline):**\n- Debounced auto-save (800ms) on any input change → POST to `/workout/<id>/log`\n- Manual save button → same endpoint\n- Add exercise → POST to `/workout/<id>/exercise`, append card to DOM\n- Remove exercise → DELETE, remove card from DOM with confirm\n- Finish workout → save first, then POST status=completed\n\n### 4. `argus/fitness_web/static/style.css` — Workout styles\n\n- `.exercise-card` — white bg, rounded border, 1rem padding\n- `.exercise-header` — flex with name, target, x button\n- `.variation-badge` — gray pill for non-standard variations\n- `.set-row` — flex with inputs, 44px min-height for mobile\n- `.superset-block` — orange left border, grouped padding\n- `.add-exercise-btn` — full width, dashed border, \"+ Add Exercise\"\n- `.workout-actions` — flex with save + finish buttons\n- `.modal` — fixed overlay, bottom sheet on mobile\n- All inputs: centered text, focus border orange (#fc4c02)\n\n### 5. `argus/fitness_web/templates/index.html` — Add workouts section\n\nAdd a \"Workouts\" table above the existing Activities table showing recent workouts (date, title, status badge, click-through to `/workout/<id>`).\n\n### 6. `argus/src/tools.py` — `create_workout` tool\n\n**Tool definition:**\n- `title`: string (required)\n- `exercises`: array of {name, variation, target_sets, target_reps, target_duration_seconds, target_rpe, target_weight, superset_group, notes, rest_seconds, muscle_group}\n- `notes`: string (optional)\n\n**Execution:** calls `fitness_db.create_workout_with_exercises()`, returns `\"Workout created: https://fit.argbox.net/workout/{id}\"`\n\nAlso add `log_measurement` tool (type, value, unit).\n\n### 7. **Detailed instructions** — the worker needs a proper domain contract, not just a one-liner in chat.md. Like a `domains/fitness/CONTEXT.md` with your program structure, exercise progressions, how to program sets/reps for your level, rest days, etc.

- Progression scheme (when to add reps, when to add sets, when to move to harder variations)

# Calisthenics Training Program Research -- Comprehensive Summary

## 1. Beginner-to-Intermediate Calisthenics Programming

### Best Split for This Level

For someone who can do 1-2 pull-ups and 11 push-ups, the unanimous recommendation from Steven Low (Overcoming Gravity), the r/bodyweightfitness community, and Hybrid Calisthenics is **full-body training 3 days per week** on non-consecutive days (e.g., Mon/Wed/Fri). The reasoning: beginners adapt quickly to exercise, recover faster than they think, and benefit from hitting each movement pattern 3x/week rather than 1x with a split. Upper/lower or push/pull/legs splits become appropriate only after 6-12 months of consistent training when volume needs exceed what a full-body session can handle.

### Program Structure (per Steven Low / Overcoming Gravity)

Each session should include:
1. **Warm-up / mobility** (10-15 min)
2. **Skill work** (5-15 min) -- handstand practice, L-sit work
3. **Strength pairs** (main work) -- 2 pushing + 2 pulling exercises, paired as supersets
4. **Legs** (2 exercises)
5. **Cool-down / flexibility** (5-10 min)

Pick **6 main exercises**: 2 vertical/horizontal push, 2 vertical/horizontal pull, 2 legs. Pair opposing movements (e.g., push-ups with rows, dips with pull-ups) with 1.5-3.5 min rest between pairs.

### Progressive Overload in Calisthenics

Unlike weights where you add load, calisthenics progressive overload works through:
- **Harder variations** (incline push-up to standard to decline to diamond to archer)
- **Increased leverage** (tucked to extended positions)
- **Added time under tension** (slower tempo, pauses)
- **Reduced stability** (floor to rings)
- **Volume increases** (more reps/sets before moving to harder variation)

The key rule from multiple sources: **when you can do 3 sets of 8 reps with good form, move to the next harder variation**, dropping back to 3 sets of 5 reps.

### Sources
- [Steven Low - Fundamentals of Bodyweight Strength Training](https://stevenlow.org/the-fundamentals-of-bodyweight-strength-training/)
- [Calisthenics Association - 30-Day Beginner Program](https://calisthenicsassociation.org/blog/calisthenics-30-day-beginner-program)
- [Fitloop - Progressive Overload for Calisthenics](https://www.fitloop.app/guides/progressive-overload-calisthenics)
- [Hybrid Calisthenics](https://www.hybridcalisthenics.com/routine)

---

## 2. Pull-Up Progression Programs (1-2 to 10+)

### Current Status Assessment

With 1.5 pull-ups, he is at the critical "first pull-up" stage. The priority is building volume while maintaining full range of motion.

### Approach A: Combined Volume Method (Best for 1-3 rep max)

From Nerd Fitness and Cali Move, the recommended approach:
- Do 1 full pull-up, then continue the set with **negative pull-ups** (3-5 second lowering)
- Aim for sets of 1 full + 3-4 negatives
- 3 sets, 3x/week
- Supplement with **inverted rows** (3x8-12) and **dead hangs** (3x20-30s)
- When you can do 3 full pull-ups per set, shift to pure full pull-ups and build volume

### Approach B: Greasing the Groove (GTG)

Popularized by Pavel Tsatsouline, this is highly effective for pull-ups:
- Perform **50% of your max** throughout the day (so 1 rep at a time with 1.5 max)
- **3-5 mini-sessions** per day, spread 1-2+ hours apart
- **Never go to failure** -- every rep should be crisp
- Practice daily
- **Retest max every 2-3 weeks** and adjust working reps
- Expected results: significant increase within 1-2 months

For someone at 1-2 reps, GTG means doing **singles** spread throughout the day, 5-8 times. This builds neurological efficiency without fatiguing the muscles.

### Approach C: Fighter Pull-Up Program (Pavel Tsatsouline)

For a 3-rep max starting point (which he should reach quickly):
- 5 sets per day in descending rep pattern: 3, 2, 1, 1, 1
- Each day, add a rep to the last set, then propagate forward
- 5 training days on, 1 day off
- After 12 days at 3RM protocol, transition to 5RM protocol
- **Expected results: 2.5-3x increase** (5 reps becomes 12-15 reps in ~30 days)

### Approach D: Armstrong Pull-Up Program (5-day structure)

- **Day 1**: 5 max-effort sets, 90s rest between sets
- **Day 2**: Pyramid (1, 2, 3... until failure), 10s rest per rep in previous set
- **Day 3**: 9 training sets (3 overhand, 3 underhand, 3 wide), 60s rest
- **Day 4**: Max number of training sets possible, 60s rest
- **Day 5**: Repeat hardest day of the week
- Also includes: 3 sets of max push-ups every morning
- Timeline: 6-8 weeks for significant gains

### Recommendation for This Person

Start with **GTG for 4-6 weeks** (daily singles/doubles) combined with **3x/week structured pull-up work** (negatives + assisted + rows). Once he hits 5 clean pull-ups, switch to the **Fighter Pull-Up Program** for the push to 10+.

### Sources
- [Nerd Fitness - Do a Pull-Up](https://www.nerdfitness.com/blog/do-a-pull-up/)
- [Pavel's Fighter Pull-Up Program](https://www.kbnj.com/FighterPullupByPavel.htm)
- [Breaking Muscle - Greasing the Groove](https://breakingmuscle.com/greasing-the-groove/)
- [Pull-Up Solution - Armstrong Program](https://thepullupsolution.com/blog/the-armstrong-pullup-program-complete-review)
- [Cali Move - Pull-Ups for Beginners](https://blog.calimove.com/2024/01/26/pull-ups-for-beginners-how-to-get-from-0-to-5-pull-ups/)

---

## 3. Push-Up Progression (11 to 35-45)

### Volume Building Phase (11 to 25 reps)

The most effective approach from multiple sources:
- **3 sets to near-failure**, 3x/week (48 hours between sessions)
- Rest **1-2 minutes** between sets
- Track total reps per workout and aim to increase weekly
- Use the "Hundred Push-Ups" graduated approach: test max, follow the appropriate column
- **GTG can work here too**: do sets of 6-7 (50-60% of max) throughout the day, 5-8 times

Jim Stoppani's Push-Up Density method: perform 40 reps in progressively shorter timeframes. Start with 4 sets of 10 in 10 minutes, work toward 4 sets of 10 in 4 minutes.

### When to Add Harder Variations

Once standard push-ups hit **20-25 clean reps**, begin introducing variations for the strength stimulus while maintaining standard push-ups for volume:
- **20+ reps**: Add **diamond push-ups** (closer hand placement, more tricep emphasis)
- **25+ reps**: Add **decline push-ups** (feet elevated, more shoulder/upper chest)
- **30+ reps**: Add **archer push-ups** (one-arm dominant, unilateral strength)
- **35+ reps**: Add **weighted push-ups** or **ring push-ups** for continued progression

### Programming for 11 to 45

A practical progression:
- **Weeks 1-4**: 4 sets of max reps (standard push-ups), 90s rest, 3x/week. Target: reach 15-18 reps per set
- **Weeks 5-8**: 3 sets standard + 2 sets diamond, 3x/week. Target: 20+ standard
- **Weeks 9-12**: Mixed sets (standard, diamond, decline), 3x/week. Target: 25-30 standard
- **Weeks 13-16**: Continue with harder variations as primary, standard for test. Target: 35+

### Sources
- [Hundred Pushups Program](https://hundredpushups.com/)
- [Jim Stoppani - Push-Up Density](https://www.jimstoppani.com/training/push-up-density-training-program)
- [StrongFirst - Plans for Building Powerful Pushups](https://www.strongfirst.com/plans-for-building-powerful-pushups/)
- [BarBend - Beginner Push-Up Program](https://barbend.com/beginner-push-up-program/)

---

## 4. Running Programming (33min 5K to sub-23)

### The Journey: Three Phases

This is a ~10-minute improvement, which is substantial. It requires phased progression over 6-12 months.

**Phase 1: Build Aerobic Base (Weeks 1-12) -- 33:00 to ~28:00**
- 3 runs/week, all at easy/conversational pace
- Start at 3x 2-3km, build to 3x 5km
- Follow Hal Higdon's Novice 5K structure
- Add walking breaks if needed -- no shame
- Goal: run 5K continuously at comfortable pace

**Phase 2: Introduce Speed Work (Weeks 13-24) -- 28:00 to ~25:00**
- 4 runs/week:
  - 2 easy runs (5-8km at conversational pace)
  - 1 interval session (400m repeats at faster than race pace, 90-120s jog recovery)
  - 1 tempo run (10min easy + 10-15min at "comfortably hard" + 5-10min easy)
- Weekly mileage: 20-30km
- Follow Hal Higdon's Intermediate 5K (8-week plan)

**Phase 3: Race-Specific Training (Weeks 25-36+) -- 25:00 to sub-23:00**
- 4-5 runs/week:
  - 2 easy runs
  - 1 interval session (6-8x 400m at ~1:40-1:45 pace, or 4-5x 800m)
  - 1 tempo run (15-20min at ~5:00-5:10/km)
  - 1 long run (10-14km at easy pace)
- Target paces: sub-23 = 4:36/km. Interval pace ~4:15-4:20/km. Easy pace ~6:00-6:30/km
- Apply 80/20 rule: 80% of running at easy effort, 20% hard

### Key Principles
- **Increase weekly volume by no more than 10% per week**
- **48 hours between hard efforts** (intervals or tempo)
- Easy runs should genuinely feel easy -- conversational pace
- Long runs build aerobic capacity that supports speed work
- Measurable improvement appears within 6-8 weeks of structured training

### How Running Fits with Calisthenics
- Separate hard running from hard strength days by at least 24 hours
- Easy runs can go on the same day as calisthenics (run in morning, calisthenics in evening, or vice versa)
- If same-day, do strength first when maximum force production matters, then cardio after

## 5. Body Recomposition

### Why This Person Is an Ideal Candidate

Three factors make body recomposition highly achievable:
1. **Detrained with muscle memory** (3 years gym history) -- myonuclear domain theory suggests muscle regains faster than initial gains
2. **24.1% body fat** -- enough stored energy to fuel muscle growth in a deficit
3. **Relative beginner to calisthenics** -- novel stimulus drives rapid adaptation

### Caloric Intake

Research consensus:
- **Small deficit of 200-300 calories below maintenance** is optimal for recomposition
- Muscle growth comes to a halt once deficits surpass ~300 calories
- Muscle begins to decrease with deficits larger than 500 calories
- For a 75.3kg male, maintenance is roughly 2200-2500 kcal (activity-dependent)
- Target: **~2000-2200 kcal/day** (with adjustment based on training days)
- Maximum safe rate: **0.7% of bodyweight per week** loss (~0.5kg/week)

### Protein Requirements

Strong research consensus:
- **Minimum: 1.6g/kg bodyweight** (120g/day for 75.3kg)
- **Optimal range: 2.0-2.4g/kg** (150-180g/day) for recomposition in a deficit
- Studies found that subjects consuming ~1g/lb (2.2g/kg) gained muscle while losing fat, while low-protein groups (~0.4g/lb) lost muscle despite fat loss
- Distribute across 4-5 meals for optimal muscle protein synthesis

### Other Macros
- **Fat**: 0.8-1.0g/kg (60-75g/day) -- supports hormones, especially important for Pitta-Vata constitution
- **Carbs**: fill remaining calories (~200-250g/day on training days, ~150-180g on rest days)

### Timeline

At 75.3kg and 24.1% body fat:
- Current lean mass: ~57.2kg, fat mass: ~18.1kg
- Target 12% body fat at estimated ~70kg: lean mass ~61.6kg, fat mass ~8.4kg
- This means: gain ~4.4kg muscle, lose ~9.7kg fat
- Realistic timeline: **12-18 months** with consistent training and nutrition
- Expect faster early progress (1-2 lb muscle/month as detrained beginner) slowing over time

### Sources
- [Built With Science - Body Recomposition](https://builtwithscience.com/diet/meal-plans/body-recomposition/)
- [BodySpec - Building Muscle in a Calorie Deficit](https://www.bodyspec.com/blog/post/building_muscle_in_a_calorie_deficit_how_to_achieve_body_recomposition)
- [Transparent Labs - 8-Week Recomp Guide](https://www.transparentlabs.com/blogs/all/body-recomposition-how-to-lose-fat-and-gain-muscle)
- [Healthline - Body Recomposition](https://www.healthline.com/nutrition/body-recomposition)

## 6. Weekly Schedule Design

### Key Constraints
- Must accommodate: calisthenics (3x), running (3-4x), yoga/flexibility (2-3x)
- Must have: 1-2 full rest days
- Must avoid: hard strength + hard cardio on same day
- Must observe: 48 hours between hard efforts of same type

### Concurrent Training Research Findings

From the meta-analyses and Barbell Medicine:
- Concurrent training does **not** compromise muscle hypertrophy or maximal strength when properly programmed
- **Explosive strength** gains may be attenuated when aerobic and strength training are in the same session
- Interference is greatest when there is **<20 minutes** between modalities in the same session
- Ideally separate strength and cardio by **3-6 hours** if on the same day
- If same-day is unavoidable, **do strength first** (requires more neural freshness)
- High volumes of endurance work combined with frequent lifting increases interference risk

### Recommended Weekly Template

**Option A: 6-Day Schedule (Recommended)**

| Day | AM | PM |
|-----|----|----|
| Monday | Calisthenics (Full Body) | -- |
| Tuesday | Easy Run (30-40 min) | Yoga/Flexibility (30 min) |
| Wednesday | Calisthenics (Full Body) | -- |
| Thursday | Intervals or Tempo Run | Yoga/Flexibility (30 min) |
| Friday | Calisthenics (Full Body) | -- |
| Saturday | Long Run (easy pace) | -- |
| Sunday | **REST** or gentle yoga | -- |

**Option B: 5-Day Schedule (More Recovery)**

| Day | Session |
|-----|---------|
| Monday | Calisthenics + short easy run (PM) |
| Tuesday | REST or yoga |
| Wednesday | Calisthenics + yoga/flexibility |
| Thursday | Interval/Tempo Run |
| Friday | Calisthenics |
| Saturday | Long Run + yoga/flexibility |
| Sunday | REST |

### Principles
- Never do hard calisthenics and hard running on the same day
- Yoga/flexibility pairs well with easy runs or as evening sessions after calisthenics
- Easy runs are genuine recovery -- they should not feel taxing
- Consider periodization: alternate 4-week blocks prioritizing strength vs running
- Listen to body -- Pitta-Vata constitution benefits from structured but not excessive intensity

### Sources
- [Barbell Medicine - Concurrent Training and the Interference Effect](https://www.barbellmedicine.com/blog/concurrent-training-and-the-interference-effect/)
- [PMC - Concurrent Exercise Training](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5407958/)
- [PMC - Optimizing Concurrent Training](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11688070/)
- [Scientific American - Weights vs Cardio](https://www.scientificamerican.com/article/weights-vs-cardio-keep-them-separate-or-combine/)
- [Peanut Butter Runner - Balancing Running, Strength, and Yoga](https://www.peanutbutterrunner.com/5-tips-for-balancing-running-strength-training-and-yoga/)

## 7. Exercise Progressions for Calisthenics

### PUSH (Vertical)
1. Wall push-ups
2. Incline push-ups (high surface)
3. Incline push-ups (low surface)
4. Standard push-ups
5. Diamond push-ups
6. Decline push-ups
7. Archer push-ups
8. Pseudo-planche push-ups
9. One-arm push-up progressions

### PUSH (Vertical / Overhead)
1. Pike push-ups
2. Elevated pike push-ups (feet on box)
3. Wall handstand holds
4. Wall handstand push-ups (partial ROM)
5. Wall handstand push-ups (full ROM)
6. Freestanding handstand push-ups

### PUSH (Dips)
1. Bench dips
2. Assisted parallel bar dips (band or foot on box)
3. Parallel bar dips
4. Ring support hold (15s prerequisite for ring work)
5. Ring dips (band-assisted)
6. Ring dips
7. Weighted ring dips

### PULL (Vertical)
1. Dead hang (30-60s)
2. Scapular pulls (active hang)
3. Inverted rows (high angle)
4. Inverted rows (low angle / Australian pull-ups)
5. Negative pull-ups (3-5s lowering)
6. Band-assisted pull-ups
7. Full pull-ups (chin-ups are slightly easier)
8. L-sit pull-ups
9. Archer pull-ups
10. Weighted pull-ups

### PULL (Horizontal)
1. Inverted rows (feet on ground, body at 45 degrees)
2. Inverted rows (feet on ground, body horizontal)
3. Inverted rows (feet elevated)
4. Front lever tuck holds
5. Advanced tuck front lever
6. Front lever (full)

### SQUAT
1. Chair squats (sit to chair)
2. Air squats
3. Jump squats
4. Split squats
5. Bulgarian split squats (rear foot elevated)
6. Shrimp squat progressions
7. Pistol squat progressions (assisted to full)

### HINGE
1. Glute bridges
2. Single-leg glute bridges
3. Hip hinge practice (Romanian deadlift pattern)
4. Nordic curl negatives
5. Nordic curls
6. Single-leg Nordic curls

### CORE
1. Dead bug (10-15 reps)
2. Forearm plank (30-60s)
3. Side plank (30-60s each side)
4. Hollow body hold (20-30s)
5. Lying leg raises
6. Hanging knee raises
7. L-sit (tucked, 5-20s)
8. L-sit (one leg extended)
9. L-sit (full, target 15-30s)
10. Hanging leg raises (toes to bar)
11. Dragon flags

## 8. Sets, Reps, and Rest Schemes

### By Training Goal

| Parameter | Strength | Hypertrophy | Endurance |
|-----------|----------|-------------|-----------|
| Reps per set | 3-5 | 8-12 | 15-25+ |
| Sets per exercise | 3-5 | 3-5 | 2-3 |
| Rest between sets | 3-5 min | 60-120s | 30-60s |
| Tempo | Controlled, explosive concentric | 2-3s each phase | Fast, sustained |
| Total reps/session (per movement) | 25-50 | 40-75 | 50-100+ |

Source: Steven Low's Prilepin tables adapted for bodyweight.

### Practical Application for This Person

Since goals are both strength (pull-ups, push-ups) and body composition (recomp), a hybrid approach:
- **Pull-ups / harder exercises**: 3-5 sets of 3-5 reps (strength focus) with 3-5 min rest
- **Push-ups / moderate exercises**: 3-4 sets of 8-12 reps (hypertrophy focus) with 90-120s rest
- **Core / holds**: 3-5 sets of max hold time (targeting 60-70% of max), 60-90s rest

### Volume Per Muscle Group Per Week

For beginners:
- **10-15 sets per muscle group per week** is sufficient
- With full-body 3x/week: 3-5 sets per movement per session = 9-15 total weekly sets
- This aligns with research showing beginners respond well to moderate volume
- As training age increases (6-12 months), volume can increase to 15-20+ sets/week

### Key Principle
Steven Low emphasizes: "We are not working to failure. Set and repetition selection should be based on completing the first set with a repetition or two to spare." Leave 1-2 reps in reserve (RIR) on most sets. Failure training increases recovery demands without proportional benefit for beginners.

### Paired Sets (Supersets)

Pair push + pull exercises to save time and improve recovery:
- Set of push-ups, rest 90s, set of rows, rest 90s, repeat
- This allows each muscle group ~3 min effective rest while keeping sessions under 60 min

### Sources
- [Steven Low - Prilepin Tables for Bodyweight](https://stevenlow.org/prilepin-tables-for-bodyweight-strength-isometric-and-eccentric-exercises/)
- [Steven Low - Fundamentals of Bodyweight Strength Training](https://stevenlow.org/the-fundamentals-of-bodyweight-strength-training/)
- [MP Calisthenics - How to Structure Your Workout](https://www.mpcalisthenics.com/tutorial/how-to-structure-your-calisthenics-workout-for-maximum-results)
- [Bar Brothers - Guide to Repetitions](https://www.barbrothersgroningen.com/repetitions-strength-mass-endurance/)
- [Calisteniapp - Rest Times in Calisthenics](https://calisteniapp.com/articles/Rest-Times-in-Calisthenics:-How-Long-You-Should-Rest-Based-on-Your-Goals)

## 9. Deload and Recovery

### How Often

- **Beginners: every 8-10 weeks** of consistent training
- More experienced: every 4-6 weeks (per Steven Low's recommendation of every 4-8 weeks)
- Also deload when: progress stalls, fatigue accumulates beyond normal, joints ache, motivation drops, sleep quality declines

### What a Deload Week Looks Like

- **Cut total sets and reps by 40-60%**
- If normally doing 5 sets of 10 push-ups, reduce to 2-3 sets
- Maintain exercise selection but drop to **easier progressions** (e.g., if doing diamond push-ups, drop to standard)
- Keep the same training schedule (3x/week) but with dramatically reduced volume
- **Maintain intensity** (don't make exercises too easy -- just do fewer sets)
- Running: cut to 50-60% of normal weekly mileage, all easy pace

### Active Recovery Options During Deload
- Light yoga / stretching sessions
- Easy walking
- Basic mobility work
- Dead hangs and gentle joint rotations
- Focus on flexibility and body control

### Benefits Specific to Calisthenics
- Tendons and ligaments heal (connective tissue adapts slower than muscle)
- Neuromuscular coordination improves (skill consolidation)
- Prevents overuse injuries common in high-rep bodyweight work (wrist, shoulder, elbow)

### Signs You Need an Unscheduled Deload
- Strength regression for 2+ sessions
- Persistent joint pain (not just muscle soreness)
- Elevated resting heart rate (89 bpm is already on the higher side -- monitor this)
- Poor sleep quality despite good habits
- Loss of motivation and training feeling like a chore

### Sources
- [A Shot of Adrenaline - Complete Guide to Deloading for Bodyweight Training](https://ashotofadrenaline.net/deloading/)
- [Legion Athletics - Deload Weeks](https://legionathletics.com/deload-week/)
- [Antranik - Importance of Rest Days and Deload Weeks](https://antranik.org/the-importance-of-rest-days-and-deload-weeks/)
- [Gymshark - What is a Deload Week](https://row.gymshark.com/blog/article/deload-week)

## 10. Warm-Up and Cool-Down

### Complete Warm-Up Protocol (10-15 minutes)

**Phase 1: General Cardio Activation (2-3 min)**
- Jumping jacks: 30-45 seconds
- High knees: 30 seconds
- Butt kicks: 30 seconds
- Light jogging or skipping rope: 1-2 minutes

**Phase 2: Dynamic Stretching (5-7 min)**

Upper body:
- Arm circles (small to large): 30 seconds each direction
- Cross-body arm swings: 30 seconds
- Inchworms: 5-6 reps
- Thread the needle: 5 reps each side

Lower body:
- Leg swings front to back: 10 each leg
- Leg swings side to side: 10 each leg
- Walking lunges with rotation: 8 each side
- Deep squat hold with reach: 30 seconds

**Phase 3: Joint Mobility (3-4 min)**

Wrists (critical for calisthenics):
- Wrist circles: 10 each direction
- Prayer stretch pulses: 15 reps
- Floor wrist extensions/flexions: 10 each

Shoulders (especially important given left shoulder restriction):
- Band pull-aparts: 15 reps
- Wall slides: 10 reps
- Shoulder dislocates with band: 10 reps

Hips:
- 90/90 hip switches: 8 each side
- Hip CARs: 5 each direction per leg
- Cossack squats: 6 each side

**Phase 4: Sport-Specific Activation (2-3 min)**
- Push days: Scapular push-ups (10), incline push-ups (8-10)
- Pull days: Scapular pull-ups (8-10), dead hangs (20-30s)
- Leg days: Glute bridges (12), bodyweight squats (10)

### Cool-Down Protocol (5-10 min)
- Static stretches held **30-60 seconds** each (save static stretching for AFTER training)
- Key stretches: chest doorway stretch, lat stretch, hip flexor stretch, hamstring stretch, shoulder cross-body stretch
- Breathing exercises: diaphragmatic breathing for 2-3 minutes
- This is where the yoga/flexibility pillar overlaps -- evening yoga sessions serve as extended cool-downs

### Critical Note
Multiple studies show **static stretching before training reduces force production by 2-5%**. All pre-workout stretching should be dynamic. Save static holds for cool-down or dedicated flexibility sessions.

### Sources
- [Calisthenics Association - Best Warm-Up Routine](https://calisthenicsassociation.org/blog/best-warm-up-routine-calisthenics)
- [Calisteniapp - Calisthenics Warm-Up for Shoulders, Wrists, Elbows](https://calisteniapp.com/articles/calisthenics-warm-up-routine)
- [White Coat Trainer - 5 Minute Calisthenics Warm-Up](https://whitecoattrainer.com/blog/calisthenics-warm-up)

## Special Considerations for This Person

### Pitta-Vata Constitution
- Avoid excessive heat-building exercise (no HIIT-heavy programs initially)
- Favor structured, gradual progression over aggressive intensity
- Include cooling activities (yoga, swimming) alongside intense training
- Consistent routine matters more than maximum effort
- Adequate rest and not overtraining is critical -- Pitta-Vata types can burn out from overexertion

### Shoulder Mobility Imbalance (Left Restricted)
- Prioritize shoulder mobility in every warm-up
- Add extra band pull-aparts and shoulder dislocates on the left side
- Consider adding dedicated shoulder CARs (Controlled Articular Rotations) daily
- Monitor for pain during overhead work (handstands, pike push-ups)
- Rows and face pulls will help balance shoulder mechanics

### Resting HR of 89
- This is above average (normal adult: 60-100, fit adult: 50-70)
- Will improve significantly with aerobic base building
- Track this metric -- it should drop as fitness improves
- If it stays elevated after 8-12 weeks of cardio, consult a physician

Explain to me the GTG or whatever I don;t undersatnd

> AGENT

GTG = Greasing the Groove. It's dead simple.

The idea: **practice the movement many times a day, never going anywhere near failure**. You're training your nervous system to get better at the movement, not exhausting your muscles.

For you with pull-ups at 1.5 reps, it looks like this:

- You have a pull-up bar at home (or wherever)
- Every time you walk past it, you do **1 pull-up**. Just one. Easy, clean, no struggle.
- You do this **5-8 times throughout the day**, spread at least an hour apart
- That's it. No set structure, no rest timing, no failure.

**Why it works:** Your body gets better at things it practices frequently. By doing 5-8 singles a day, you're doing 35-56 pull-ups a week — way more volume than 3 sets to failure twice a week — but each rep is so easy that you recover fully between them. Your nervous system learns to recruit muscle fibers more efficiently.

**The rules:**
- Never go above 50% of your max (so for you: just singles)
- Every rep should feel easy — if it feels hard, stop for the day
- Spread reps throughout the day, not back to back
- Do it daily, even on "rest days" — it's not taxing enough to need recovery
- Retest your max every 2-3 weeks. When your max hits 4, start doing doubles throughout the day. When it hits 6, do triples. And so on.

People routinely double or triple their max within 4-6 weeks with this method. Pavel Tsatsouline (the guy who popularized it) had military personnel go from 5 pull-ups to 15+ in a month.

The catch: **you need a bar accessible throughout the day**. If you can only train at a park, GTG doesn't work — it's a "throughout your daily life" method. Do you have a pull-up bar at home?

# TOPIC 1: RUNNING PLAN — Beginner to Sub-23 5K

## PHASE 1: Building the Base (Current State to Running 5K Comfortably)

### Where You Are Now
You ran 3.91km in 26:19 with stops. That puts your approximate running pace around 6:44/km when moving, but with walk breaks. You're not a couch potato — you can already cover near-5K distance — but you need to build continuous running endurance.

### The Couch-to-5K Structure (NHS Version, Week by Week)

The standard C25K is 9 weeks, 3 runs/week. Here's the full NHS plan:

- **Week 1**: Run 1 min, walk 1:30 min, repeat 7 times. Total ~28 min per session.
- **Week 2**: Run 1:30, walk 2:00, repeat 5 times. Total ~29 min.
- **Week 3**: Run 1:30/walk, then run 3:00/walk, repeat. Total ~25 min.
- **Week 4**: Run 3:00, walk 1:30, run 5:00, walk 2:30, run 3:00, walk 1:30, run 5:00. Total ~31 min.
- **Week 5**: Sessions 1-2 are 5-min run intervals with 3-min walks. **Session 3 is the breakthrough: first continuous 20-minute run.**
- **Week 6**: Sessions 1-2 return to intervals, then Session 3 is a continuous 25-minute run.
- **Week 7**: All 3 runs are 25 minutes continuous.
- **Week 8**: All 3 runs are 28 minutes continuous.
- **Week 9**: All 3 runs are 30 minutes continuous. Program complete.

### Modified Plan for Your Level

Since you can already run ~4km (with stops), you don't need to start at Week 1. You should likely **start around Week 4-5** of the standard plan. Your realistic timeline to run 5K comfortably and continuously is **3-5 weeks** from now, not the full 9.

A practical approach:
- **Weeks 1-2**: Run 3x/week. Run/walk intervals: 5 min run, 2 min walk, repeat for 30 min total. Focus on keeping running pace very easy.
- **Weeks 3-4**: Run 3x/week. Run 8-10 min, walk 2 min, repeat. One session per week try a continuous 20-min run.
- **Week 5+**: All runs continuous. Aim for 25-30 min running at easy pace. By this point you should be comfortably covering 5K.

### How Many Runs Per Week

**3 runs per week** is sufficient and recommended for this phase. Always have at least one rest day between runs.

### What Pace for All Runs

**ALL runs in Phase 1 should be at conversational pace.** This means:
- You can speak in full sentences while running (not gasping single words)
- Heart rate zone: **60-70% of max heart rate** (Zone 2)
- For a 26-year-old, max HR is approximately 194 (220-26). So conversational pace = **116-136 bpm**.
- In practical terms, this will feel **embarrassingly slow** — probably 7:00-8:00+ per km. That's correct. Speed comes later.

### Injury Prevention

The biggest risks for beginners are shin splints, runner's knee, and Achilles tendonitis. Prevention strategies:

**Running Form Basics:**
- **Cadence**: Aim for 170-180 steps per minute. Shorter, quicker steps reduce impact.
- **Foot strike**: Land on your midfoot, under your center of gravity — not out in front (overstriding). Don't reach with your heel.
- **Posture**: Keep head, shoulders, and hips aligned. Slight forward lean from the ankles (not the waist). Relaxed shoulders, arms at roughly 90 degrees.
- **Don't overstride**: This is the #1 cause of shin splints and knee pain. If your foot lands ahead of your body, you're braking with every step.

**Training Rules:**
- **10% rule**: Never increase weekly mileage by more than 10% per week.
- **Rest days are non-negotiable**: Your body adapts during rest, not during running.
- **Surface variety**: Mix in softer surfaces (trails, grass) when possible.
- **Shoes**: Get proper running shoes with cushioning. Replace after 400-800 km.
- **Calf raises and glute bridges**: 3x15 each, 3x/week — these strengthen the muscles that protect your shins and knees.

---

## PHASE 2: Getting Faster (5K Comfortable to Sub-28)

### When to Introduce Speed Work

Only after you can run 5K continuously and comfortably for at least **4-6 weeks** at easy pace. Your body needs time to build connective tissue strength. Rushing into speed work on a weak aerobic base is the most common cause of injury.

### Types of Runs Explained

**Easy Runs**
- Pace: Conversational, 60-70% max HR. About 1:30-2:00/km slower than your goal 5K pace.
- Purpose: Build aerobic base, promote recovery, develop fat-burning efficiency and mitochondrial density.
- Duration: 25-40 minutes.
- These should be the majority of your running.

**Tempo Runs**
- Pace: "Comfortably hard" — you can say short phrases but not hold a conversation. About 30 seconds per km slower than your 5K race pace. For a sub-28 target (5:36/km pace), tempo pace would be around 6:00-6:10/km.
- Duration: 15-20 minutes of tempo effort (plus warm-up and cool-down).
- Purpose: Raise your lactate threshold — the pace at which lactate starts accumulating faster than you can clear it. This directly translates to being able to hold faster paces for longer.
- Frequency: Once per week.

**Interval Training**
- 400m repeats: Run 400m at faster than race pace (for sub-28: about 2:12 per 400m), jog 60-90 seconds recovery. Start with 6 repeats, build to 10.
- 800m repeats: Run 800m at slightly faster than race pace (for sub-28: about 4:29 per 800m), jog 200m recovery. Start with 3 repeats, build to 5.
- 1km repeats: Run 1km at race pace (5:36/km for sub-28), jog 90 seconds recovery. Start with 3 repeats, build to 5.
- Purpose: Improve VO2max, running economy, and ability to sustain high speeds.
- Frequency: Once per week. Never do intervals on consecutive days.

**Long Runs**
- Pace: Easy/conversational. Same as easy runs.
- Distance: 6-10km for 5K training. Under 1 hour.
- Purpose: Build aerobic endurance, mitochondrial density, and mental toughness. Strengthen tendons and connective tissue.
- Frequency: Once per week (usually Sunday).

**Fartlek ("Speed Play")**
- Swedish for "speed play." A run where you alternate between faster and slower segments by feel — no strict intervals.
- Example beginner fartlek: Warm up 5 min easy. Then do 5 rounds of 30 seconds hard / 90 seconds easy. Cool down 5 min easy.
- More advanced: 2 min hard / 2 min easy, 3 min hard / 2 min easy, 4 min hard / 2 min easy, then back down.
- Purpose: Teaches your body to shift gears. Less mentally intimidating than formal intervals. Good introduction to speed work.
- Frequency: Can replace one interval session per week.

### The 80/20 Rule

**80% of your weekly running should be at easy/conversational pace. Only 20% should be at moderate-to-hard effort (tempo, intervals, fartlek).**

This is the single most important principle. Most beginners run their easy runs too fast and their hard runs too slow, ending up in a "gray zone" that produces mediocre results and high injury risk.

Example: If you run 4 times per week totaling 25 km, only about 5 km should be at tempo/interval effort. The remaining 20 km should be genuinely easy.

### Sample Week with 3-4 Runs (Sub-28 Phase)

| Day | Workout |
|-----|---------|
| Monday | Rest |
| Tuesday | Intervals: Warm up 10 min easy. 6x400m at 2:12 with 90s jog recovery. Cool down 10 min easy. |
| Wednesday | Rest or cross-train (calisthenics) |
| Thursday | Easy run: 30-35 min at conversational pace |
| Friday | Rest |
| Saturday | Tempo: 10 min warm up. 15-20 min at tempo pace. 10 min cool down. |
| Sunday | Long run: 45-55 min at easy pace |

---

## PHASE 3: Sub-23 5K (Intermediate Training)

### Target Pace
Sub-23 requires maintaining **4:36 per km** (7:24 per mile) for the entire race.

### Required Weekly Mileage
**25-35 km per week** (15-22 miles). You should be running **4-5 times per week** at this level. Running only 3 times makes it much harder to achieve this goal.

### Benchmark Tests (Are You Ready?)
Before starting sub-23 training:
- **3x1km repeats test**: Can you run each rep in 4:30-4:35 with 2-3 min recovery?
- **1-mile time trial**: Can you run a mile in about 7:00-7:10?

If you can't hit these, spend more time in Phase 2.

### Typical Training Week at Sub-23 Level

| Day | Workout |
|-----|---------|
| Monday | Easy run: 5-6 km at conversational pace |
| Tuesday | Intervals: 5x1km at 4:25-4:30/km with 90s jog recovery |
| Wednesday | Easy run: 5-6 km + strength/mobility (15-20 min) |
| Thursday | Tempo run: 10 min warm up, 20 min at ~4:50/km, 10 min cool down |
| Friday | Rest |
| Saturday | Easy run: 5 km at conversational pace |
| Sunday | Long run: 10-12 km at easy pace (50-65 min) |

### Race-Specific Workouts
- **Goal pace repeats**: 5x1km at 4:36/km (exact race pace) with 90s recovery. This teaches your body what race pace feels like.
- **Progressive tempo**: Start at easy pace, increase to tempo, finish last 5 min at race pace.
- **Race simulation**: Run 4km at goal pace with short warm-up and cool-down.

### How Long Does Each Phase Take?

Realistic timeline from your current 33:00 estimated 5K:
- **Phase 1 (Base building)**: 3-5 weeks to run 5K comfortably
- **Phase 2 (Sub-28)**: 8-16 weeks of structured training
- **Phase 3 (Sub-23)**: Another 12-20+ weeks of increased mileage and intensity

**Total realistic timeline from 33:00 to sub-23: 6-12 months** with consistent training. This is not slow — going from 33:00 to 23:00 is a massive improvement (dropping 30% of your time).

---

## GENERAL RUNNING QUESTIONS

### Running + Calisthenics: The Interference Effect

Current research shows the "interference effect" (where cardio reduces strength gains) is **less concerning than previously thought**, particularly when:
- You separate running and strength training by at least **6 hours** (ideal) or do them on different days
- You keep running duration under **30 minutes** on strength training days
- If same day: **do calisthenics first, run after** — running before strength training reduces force production more than the reverse
- Running interferes slightly more than cycling due to eccentric muscle loading

**Practical recommendation**: Do calisthenics and running on separate days when possible. If you must combine, do calisthenics first, then a short easy run. Keep your hard running days and hard calisthenics days separate.

### Progress Tracking — What Metrics Matter

Focus on these and nothing else:
1. **Weekly mileage** — track it, increase by max 10%/week
2. **Easy pace at same heart rate** — if your easy pace gets faster at the same HR over months, your aerobic fitness is improving
3. **Resting heart rate** — measure upon waking. A declining trend = improving cardiovascular fitness
4. **Time trial results** — test a timed 1km or 1-mile every 4-6 weeks to benchmark progress
5. **RPE (Rate of Perceived Exertion)** — did today's run feel easier than the same run 4 weeks ago?

Don't obsess over daily pace — it varies with heat, fatigue, sleep, and stress.

### Breathing Techniques

- **Easy runs**: Use a 3:3 breathing rhythm (inhale 3 steps, exhale 3 steps) or 3:2 pattern.
- **Harder efforts**: Switch to 2:2 or 2:1 as intensity increases.
- **Diaphragmatic breathing**: Breathe into your belly, not your chest. This maximizes lung capacity.
- **Mouth + nose**: Inhale through both nose and mouth, exhale through mouth. Pure nasal breathing works for easy pace only.
- **Avoid**: Shallow chest breathing. If you're gasping, you're running too fast.

### Running in Heat (Pitta Considerations)

- **Run early morning (before 7am) or evening (after 6pm)** — avoid midday heat entirely.
- **Hydrate before, during, and after** — Pitta types sweat a lot and lose electrolytes faster.
- **Cooling pranayama post-run**: Sheetali (roll tongue into tube, inhale through it) or Sheetkari (inhale through clenched teeth) for an instant cool-down.
- **Coconut water** is ideal post-run hydration for Pitta.
- **Expect 10-15% slower paces in heat** — this is normal physiology, not failure.
- **Cooling towel on neck** during or after runs.
- Ayurveda suggests exercising to only **50% of your capacity** — until a mild sweat forms on forehead, underarms, and along the spine.

# TOPIC 2: YOGA / FLEXIBILITY FOR CALISTHENICS ATHLETES

## WHICH TYPE OF YOGA IS BEST?

### Comparison of Styles

**Hatha Yoga**
- Slow-paced, foundational. Individual poses held for 30-60 seconds.
- Best for: Learning alignment, building body awareness, beginners.
- For calisthenics: Builds understanding of body mechanics. Good starting point.

**Vinyasa Yoga**
- Flowing sequences linking breath to movement. Dynamic and continuous.
- Best for: Building stamina, endurance, active flexibility.
- For calisthenics: Complements training by improving movement flow and stamina. Good as a warm-up style.

**Ashtanga Yoga**
- Fixed sequence of poses, rigorous and demanding. Follows the same sequence every time.
- Best for: Building strength-endurance, discipline.
- For calisthenics: The physical demands align well with calisthenics goals (muscular endurance, sustained effort). However, it can be quite intense and may compete for recovery resources.

**Yin Yoga**
- Passive stretches held for 3-5 minutes (up to 10 minutes). Targets deep connective tissue (fascia, ligaments, tendons).
- Best for: Deep flexibility gains, recovery, releasing fascial tension.
- For calisthenics: **This is the best style for recovery and flexibility specifically.** After intense calisthenics sessions, yin is "the soothing balm for those muscles."

### The Recommendation

**For your situation (calisthenics athlete, can't touch toes, shoulder imbalance), use a combination:**
- **Yin yoga** 2x/week for deep flexibility work and recovery (rest days or evening after training)
- **Hatha or gentle Vinyasa** 2-3x/week as short daily mobility routines (10-15 min)
- **Surya Namaskar (Sun Salutations)** as a dynamic warm-up before calisthenics sessions

### "Traditional Yoga" as a Pillar

Traditional yoga encompasses the full eight limbs (Ashtanga in the classical sense — not the modern exercise style). For a calisthenics athlete treating it as a "pillar," Hatha is the closest modern equivalent to the traditional physical practice. It emphasizes proper alignment, breath control (pranayama), and meditative quality — not just stretching.

### Yoga for Flexibility vs Recovery vs Own Practice

- **Flexibility**: Yin yoga + static holds. Long holds (60-300 seconds), passive, targeting connective tissue.
- **Recovery**: Restorative yoga or gentle yin. Very passive, lots of props (bolsters, blocks), minimal effort. Activates parasympathetic nervous system.
- **Own practice**: Hatha or Ashtanga. Builds strength, balance, and awareness alongside flexibility. This is yoga as a discipline, not just a tool.

---

## FLEXIBILITY PROGRAMMING

### Frequency
- **Minimum effective dose**: 3x/week for measurable flexibility gains.
- **Optimal**: 4-5x/week. Daily short sessions (10-15 min) are better than 2 long weekly sessions.
- **Research finding**: "Weekly yoga practice is sufficient to evoke improvements in flexibility, though more frequent sessions yield better results."

### Session Duration
- **Daily maintenance**: 10-15 minutes (post-workout or morning routine)
- **Dedicated flexibility sessions**: 30-45 minutes (on rest days or dedicated yoga days)
- **Deep yin sessions**: 45-60 minutes (1-2x/week on rest days)
- **Research**: "Daily stretching durations between ten minutes and 60 minutes induce significant improvements in range of motion."

### Best Time of Day
- **Morning**: Body is stiffer but you build the habit. Good for gentle mobility flow and Surya Namaskar.
- **Post-workout**: Muscles are warm, you'll get deeper into stretches. Best time for static flexibility work.
- **Evening**: Best for yin yoga and deep stretching. Promotes sleep quality. Body is most flexible in the evening.
- **Never**: Don't do deep yin yoga or heavy static stretching BEFORE intense exercise — it activates the parasympathetic system and relaxes muscles, reducing performance.

### Training Days vs Rest Days
- **Training days**: Short 10-15 min mobility routine post-workout. Focus on areas worked (e.g., shoulder openers after pull-up day).
- **Rest days**: Longer 30-60 min dedicated yoga/flexibility session. This is where deep yin or a full Hatha class fits.
- **Both**: You can do yoga on both, just adjust intensity and duration.
- Reserve at least **one day per week** that is fully free from all training.

---

## KEY AREAS TO TARGET

### 1. Hamstrings (Can't Touch Toes)

**Why it matters**: Hamstring flexibility is essential for L-sits, pike compressions, forward folds, and general lower back health.

**Best stretches:**
- **Standing Forward Fold (Uttanasana)**: Keep knees slightly bent. Hold 30-60 seconds.
- **Seated Forward Fold (Paschimottanasana)**: Sit tall, hinge from hips (not rounding spine). Hold 60-120 seconds.
- **Supine Hamstring Stretch (with strap)**: Lie on back, loop strap around foot, straighten leg toward ceiling. Hold 60-90 seconds each side.
- **Downward Dog (Adho Mukha Svanasana)**: Pedal the feet. Great dynamic stretch.
- **Single-leg forward fold (Janu Sirsasana)**: One leg straight, one bent. Hold 60 seconds each side.

**Timeline to touch toes:**
- **2-3 weeks**: You'll start feeling looser.
- **4-8 weeks**: Visible, measurable gains with consistent 5x/week practice.
- **3-6 months**: Most people achieve touching toes with consistent daily stretching of 10-15 minutes.
- **Key**: Always warm up before stretching (5-10 min light movement). Cold static stretching is less effective and riskier.

### 2. Shoulders (Left Side Restricted)

**Why it matters**: Pull-ups, handstands, dips, skin-the-cats — virtually every upper body calisthenics movement demands shoulder mobility.

**Specific poses and stretches:**
- **Thread the Needle**: From all fours, slide one arm under the other, lowering that shoulder to the floor. Hold 30-60 seconds each side. Great for thoracic rotation AND shoulder opening.
- **Eagle Arms (Garudasana arms)**: Cross elbows, wrap forearms. Lift elbows to shoulder height. Targets posterior shoulder. Hold 30 seconds each side.
- **Cow Face Arms (Gomukhasana arms)**: One arm reaches up and behind, the other reaches down and behind, trying to clasp hands. Use a strap if hands don't meet. Hold 30 seconds each side. **This will directly reveal and address the left shoulder restriction.**
- **Shoulder Flexion Stretch**: Kneel with arms extended overhead on the floor. Press chest toward floor. Hold 30-60 seconds.
- **Shoulder Extension Stretch**: Sit with hands behind you, fingers pointing back. Slide hips forward. Opens chest and anterior shoulders.
- **Puppy Pose (Uttana Shishosana)**: Like child's pose but with hips over knees and arms extended. Intense shoulder opener.
- **Supported Fish Pose (with block)**: Place a block between shoulder blades, arms out to sides. Hold 2-3 minutes. Heart opener that reverses forward hunching.

**For the left shoulder specifically**: Do all bilateral stretches. Then add **extra time on the left side** (1.5x the hold time you use on the right). Don't force it — consistent gentle work beats aggressive pushing.

### 3. Hips

**Why it matters**: Squats (pistol squat requires deep hip flexion), L-sits (require hip flexor strength AND hamstring flexibility), and overall lower body movement.

**Key stretches:**
- **Pigeon Pose (Eka Pada Rajakapotasana)**: The king of hip openers. Hold 2-3 minutes each side in yin style.
- **Lizard Pose (Utthan Pristhasana)**: Deep lunge with forearms on the floor. Opens hip flexors.
- **90/90 Stretch**: Both knees at 90 degrees, lean forward with flat back. Excellent for hip internal/external rotation.
- **Deep Squat Hold (Malasana)**: Hold a deep squat with heels down, elbows pressing knees out. 60 seconds. You mentioned deep squat is easy — use this as a maintenance exercise.
- **Butterfly Pose (Baddha Konasana)**: Soles of feet together, knees dropping to sides. Hold 2-3 minutes. Inner thigh and groin opener.
- **Figure Four Stretch**: Lying on back, ankle crossed over opposite knee, pull bottom knee toward chest. 60 seconds each side.

### 4. Wrists

**Why it matters**: Absolutely critical for handstand work, planche progressions, and L-sits. Wrist injuries are common in calisthenics when flexibility is neglected.

**Essential wrist warm-up routine (do before EVERY calisthenics session):**
1. **Wrist circles**: 10 circles each direction, both wrists. Arms extended.
2. **Prayer stretch**: Palms together in front of chest, push hands down while keeping palms pressed. Hold 15 seconds.
3. **Kneeling wrist extensions**: On all fours, fingers pointing forward, lean forward gently over wrists. Hold 15 seconds. Then fingers pointing backward, lean back gently. Hold 15 seconds.
4. **Kneeling wrist flexions**: On all fours, place tops of hands on floor (back of hands down). Gently lean back. Hold 15 seconds.
5. **Finger lifts**: On all fours, palms flat, lift all fingers off the floor while keeping palms down. 10 reps.
6. **Wrist push-ups**: Loose fists, tops of hands on floor, do small kneeling push-ups. 5-10 reps.
7. **Fist to open**: Make tight fists, then spread fingers wide. 10 reps.

**Total time**: 3-5 minutes. Non-negotiable before handstand work.

### 5. Thoracic Spine

**Why it matters**: Limited t-spine extension means you can't get your arms fully overhead. This directly limits handstands, overhead pressing, and front lever. You need about 15 degrees of thoracic extension for full overhead range.

**Key exercises:**
- **Cat-Cow (Marjaryasana-Bitilakasana)**: The foundational t-spine mobilizer. 10 slow repetitions.
- **Lying T-Spine Rotation**: Lie on side, knees stacked and bent at 90 degrees. Top arm opens like a book to the other side. Hold 30 seconds each side.
- **Thread the Needle**: From all fours, reach one arm under your body, rotating through the thoracic spine. Hold 30 seconds each side.
- **Foam roller extension**: Lie face-up with foam roller across mid-back. Arms overhead. Extend gently over the roller. 30-60 seconds, move roller up/down the spine.
- **Child's Pose with Side Reach**: In child's pose, walk both hands to one side to get a lateral stretch through the thoracic spine. Hold 30 seconds each side.

---

## YOGA ROUTINE RECOMMENDATIONS

### Daily 15-20 Minute Routine (For Calisthenics Athletes)

Do this every day, ideally post-workout or in the morning:

1. **Cat-Cow**: 10 reps (1 min)
2. **Downward Dog** (pedal feet): 45 seconds
3. **Low Lunge (Anjaneyasana)** each side: 45 seconds each (1:30)
4. **Pigeon Pose** each side: 60 seconds each (2 min)
5. **Thread the Needle** each side: 30 seconds each (1 min)
6. **Seated Forward Fold (Paschimottanasana)**: 60 seconds
7. **Cow Face Arms (Gomukhasana arms)** each side: 30 seconds each (1 min)
8. **Supine Twist** each side: 45 seconds each (1:30)
9. **Butterfly Pose**: 60 seconds
10. **Standing Forward Fold (Uttanasana)**: 45 seconds
11. **Wrist stretches** (prayer, kneeling extensions): 1 min
12. **Supported Fish Pose** (with block or rolled towel): 2 min
13. **Savasana**: 2 min

**Total: ~16 minutes**

### Dedicated 30-45 Minute Yoga Day (Rest Days)

1. **3-5 rounds of Surya Namaskar A** (slow pace): 8-10 min
2. **Warrior I + Warrior II + Triangle** each side: 6 min
3. **Wide-legged Forward Fold**: 90 seconds
4. **Pigeon Pose** each side: 3 min each (6 min yin hold)
5. **Lizard Pose** each side: 90 seconds each (3 min)
6. **Seated Forward Fold**: 3 min (yin hold)
7. **Cow Face Pose (full — arms and legs)** each side: 2 min each (4 min)
8. **Supported Fish Pose**: 3 min
9. **Lying T-Spine Rotation** each side: 90 seconds each (3 min)
10. **Supine Hamstring Stretch (with strap)** each side: 90 seconds each (3 min)
11. **Happy Baby**: 90 seconds
12. **Savasana with Nadi Shodhana (alternate nostril breathing)**: 5 min

**Total: ~42 minutes**

### Surya Namaskar (Sun Salutation)

**Yes, it works both as a warm-up and standalone practice.**

- As a warm-up: 3-5 rounds at moderate pace before calisthenics. Takes 5-8 minutes. Warms up the entire body — shoulders, hips, hamstrings, wrists, spine.
- As standalone: 10-12 rounds at slow pace is a complete practice (~20 min). Research shows slow Surya Namaskar has similar effects to yoga training, while fast Surya Namaskar mimics aerobic exercise.
- The 12 poses flow through the same movement pattern as a burpee — it's essentially the ancient version of the same exercise.
- For a Pitta-Vata constitution: practice at a **slow to moderate pace**. Don't turn it into a competitive speed drill.

---

## YOGA FOR PITTA-VATA CONSTITUTION

### Practice Guidelines

**For the Pitta component (cooling focus):**
- Avoid competitive intensity — yoga is not a workout to "win"
- Include cooling postures: seated forward bends, wide-legged poses, supine poses
- End practice with cooling pranayama: **Sheetali** (inhale through rolled tongue) or **Nadi Shodhana** (alternate nostril breathing)
- Take frequent rest breaks. Don't overheat.
- Avoid practicing during peak sun hours (10am-4pm)
- Stay hydrated — Pitta types sweat heavily

**For the Vata component (grounding focus):**
- Hold poses longer rather than flowing quickly (more Hatha, less fast Vinyasa)
- Forward bends calm the nervous system — include them generously
- Standing and balancing poses (Tree, Warrior) connect you to the earth
- End with a long Savasana (5+ min) — Vata types need this more than anyone
- Practice in warm, comfortable environments
- Consistency of schedule matters — Vata benefits from routine and predictability

**Pranayama specifically for Pitta-Vata:**
- **Nadi Shodhana (Alternate Nostril Breathing)**: Balances both doshas. Calming for Vata, cooling for Pitta. 5-10 minutes daily.
- **Bhramari (Humming Bee Breath)**: Reduces anxiety (Vata) and irritability (Pitta).
- **Sheetali**: Cooling — specifically for Pitta. Inhale through rolled tongue, exhale through nose.
- **Avoid**: Kapalabhati and Bhastrika (too heating for Pitta, too stimulating for Vata).

---

## FLEXIBILITY PROGRESS MARKERS

### Timeline Expectations

**Month 1 (Weeks 1-4):**
- Feel noticeably looser after sessions
- Hamstrings: Can likely reach mid-shin or ankles (from mid-thigh/knee)
- Shoulders: Noticeable reduction in "stuckness" on restricted left side
- Hips: Pigeon pose becomes tolerable, not just painful
- Wrists: Increased comfort in weight-bearing positions

**Month 3 (Weeks 8-12):**
- Hamstrings: Should be approaching or touching toes
- Shoulders: Can likely clasp hands behind back (cow face pose) with a strap gap of <10cm
- Hips: Can hold pigeon comfortably, lizard pose improving
- Thoracic spine: Noticeable improvement in overhead reach
- General: Movements feel "smoother" — active flexibility improving

**Month 6:**
- Hamstrings: Touching toes with flat back should be achievable
- Shoulders: Left-right symmetry significantly improved (may not be perfect)
- Hips: Deep pigeon, lizard, and 90/90 all comfortable
- Handstand prep: Wrist and shoulder mobility sufficient for sustained handstand practice
- General: Flexibility becoming "functional" — translating to better calisthenics performance

**Month 12:**
- Full forward fold with palms on floor is realistic
- Shoulder mobility supporting advanced calisthenics (muscle-ups, skin-the-cats)
- Hip flexibility supporting pistol squats comfortably
- Yoga practice feels like a genuine skill, not just stretching

### How to Measure Progress

1. **Photo/video**: Take photos of the same poses (forward fold, pigeon, cow face arms) every 4 weeks.
2. **Sit-and-reach test**: Sit on floor, legs straight, measure how far past toes (or short of toes) your fingertips reach. Record in cm.
3. **Shoulder: Wall angel test**: Stand with back against wall, try to press arms flat against wall overhead. Measure gap.
4. **Hip: Deep squat hold time**: How long can you hold a full deep squat with heels down and upright torso?
5. **Wrist: Extension test**: On all fours, fingers pointing forward, how far can you lean forward without discomfort?

Here's the summary:

## Running

**Three phases, matching your level:**

**Phase 1 (3-5 weeks)** — You can already almost run 5K, so you skip most of C25K. Run/walk intervals 3x/week, all at conversational pace (you should be able to talk in full sentences). Within a month you should be running 5K continuously.

**Phase 2 (8-16 weeks)** — Introduce speed work, but only 20% of your running. 80% stays easy. A typical week: 1 easy run, 1 interval session (400m repeats), 1 tempo run (20 min at "comfortably hard"), 1 long run (easy pace, longer distance). This is where your 33:00 drops to ~28:00.

**Phase 3 (12-20 weeks)** — Push to sub-23. More mileage (25-35km/week), 4-5 runs, race-pace specific workouts. Your interval pace at this point would be ~4:25/km.

**Key rule**: easy runs should feel *embarrassingly* slow. Like 7:00-8:00/km. That's correct. Most beginners run too fast on easy days which kills their progress and causes injuries.

## Yoga / Flexibility

**The research says use a mix:**

- **Yin yoga** 2x/week — long passive holds (3-5 min per pose) for deep flexibility. Best on rest days or evenings. This is where you'll actually get more flexible.
- **Short daily routine** — 15-20 min of Hatha-style mobility post-workout. Covers shoulders, hamstrings, hips, wrists, t-spine.
- **Surya Namaskar** — works great as a warm-up before calisthenics (3-5 rounds, ~8 min). It's basically a full-body dynamic stretch.

**Your specific priorities:**
1. Hamstrings (can't touch toes → expect to touch toes in 3-6 months with daily work)
2. Left shoulder (extra time on that side with cow face arms, thread the needle)
3. Wrists (non-negotiable 3-5 min warm-up before every calisthenics session)
4. Hips (pigeon pose is king)

**For your Pitta-Vata constitution:** slow practice, cooling pranayama after (alternate nostril breathing), don't turn yoga into a competition, long Savasana at the end.

Program Overview\n- Three pillars: Strength (calisthenics), Cardio (running), Flexibility (yoga)\n- Flexible schedule — rotation-based, not day-locked\n- Full body 3x/week for strength, 3x/week running, daily mobility + 2x/week dedicated yoga\n- Training philosophy: gradual, structured, never to failure (leave 1-2 RIR)\n\n### 2. Current Working Levels\n- All baseline numbers with dates\n- Updated as progress is made (Argus updates this after periodic tests)\n- This is how Argus knows what to prescribe\n\n### 3. Exercise Progression Ladders\nFor each movement pattern, the full ladder from easy to hard with the \"3x8 rule\" (when you hit 3x8 clean, move to next variation, drop to 3x5):\n\n- **Push horizontal**: wall → incline → standard → diamond → decline → archer\n- **Push vertical**: pike → elevated pike → wall HSPU (partial) → wall HSPU (full)\n- **Push dips**: bench dips → assisted parallel → parallel → ring support → ring dips\n- **Pull vertical**: dead hang → scapular pulls → negatives → band-assisted → full pull-ups → L-sit pull-ups → weighted\n- **Pull horizontal**: inverted rows (high angle) → rows (low) → rows (feet elevated)\n- **Squat**: air squats → split squats → Bulgarian → shrimp → pistol progressions\n- **Hinge**: glute bridges → single-leg bridges → Nordic negatives → Nordic curls\n- **Core**: dead bug → plank → hollow hold → hanging knee raise → L-sit (tucked → one leg → full) → hanging leg raise\n\nEach exercise has: current variation, current working sets/reps, target to progress\n\n### 4. Session Templates\n- **Strength session structure**: warm-up (10-15 min incl. Surya Namaskar + wrist prep) → skill work (handstand, L-sit) → 3 paired exercises (push+pull supersets) → legs → core → cool-down\n- **Volume/intensity**: 3-5 sets of 5-8 reps for strength, 3-4 sets of 8-12 for hypertrophy, holds at 60-70% max\n- **Rest**: 90-120s between superset pairs\n- **Equipment variants**: what to do at home vs park vs gym for each exercise\n\n### 5. Running Program\n**Phase 1 (current)**: Build base — 3 easy runs/week, all conversational pace, build to continuous 5K\n**Phase 2 (after 4-6 weeks of base)**: Introduce speed — 1 easy + 1 interval + 1 tempo + 1 long run\n**Phase 3 (after sub-28)**: Sub-23 push — 4-5 runs/week, 25-35km weekly, race-pace work\n- 80/20 rule: 80% easy, 20% hard\n- Paces by phase and heart rate zones\n- Never do hard running + hard calisthenics same day\n\n### 6. Yoga / Flexibility Program\n- **Daily**: 15 min post-workout mobility routine (specific poses listed)\n- **2x/week**: 30-45 min dedicated yin yoga sessions on rest days\n- **Warm-up**: 3-5 rounds Surya Namaskar before calisthenics\n- **Priority areas**: hamstrings, left shoulder, wrists, hips, t-spine\n- **Pranayama**: Nadi Shodhana (daily), Sheetali (after hard sessions)\n\n### 7. GTG Protocol\n- Pull-ups: when at home, do singles/doubles spread through day (50% of max, 5-8x daily)\n- Rules: never to failure, every rep crisp, spread 1+ hours apart\n- Retest max every 2-3 weeks, adjust working reps\n- Can be flagged when asking for a workout: \"I'm home today\" → includes GTG\n\n### 8. Recovery Rules (for Argus to enforce)\n- Min 48 hours between hard sessions for same muscle group\n- If trained 3+ consecutive days → suggest rest day\n- If 3+ days since last workout → send nudge\n- Deload every 8-10 weeks (cut volume 40-60%, keep same exercises at easier variations)\n- Signs to auto-suggest rest: user reports poor sleep, joint pain, or motivation drop\n\n### 9. Nutrition Guidelines\n- Small deficit: 200-300 cal below maintenance (~2000-2200 kcal/day)\n- Protein: 2.0-2.4g/kg (150-180g/day)\n- Fat: 0.8-1.0g/kg (60-75g/day)\n- Carbs: fill remaining\n- Hydrate well, especially for Pitta constitution\n\n### 10. Milestones & Testing\n- Retest baseline exercises every 4-6 weeks\n- Track: max pull-ups, max push-ups, plank hold, 5K time, weight, body fat\n- Milestone targets at 3, 6, 12 months (from research)\n\n## Implementation\n\n1. Write `program.md` to vault on VPS via SSH\n2. Update `memory/project_fitness.md` with program status\n3. This file will later be read by the fitness domain contract when Argus generates workouts\n\n## Verification\n\n- Read the file back to verify it's well-structured\n- Check it covers everything needed for Argus to generate a workout with just: \"I'm at the park, got 45 minutes\"\n\n---\n\n# Phase 2: Workout Logging System"
}

The web page is just a form/tracker.\n\n## Schema Design\n\n### New tables (add to `init_db()` in fitness_db.py)\n\n**`workouts`** — session container:\n```sql\nCREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS workouts (\n    id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,\n    date TEXT NOT NULL,\n    title TEXT,\n    notes TEXT,\n    status TEXT DEFAULT 'planned',\n    created_at TEXT DEFAULT (datetime('now')),\n    completed_at TEXT\n);\n```\n\n**`workout_exercises`** — planned exercises (the AI-generated template):\n```sql\nCREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS workout_exercises (\n    id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,\n    workout_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES workouts(id),\n    exercise_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES exercises(id),\n    position INTEGER NOT NULL,\n    variation TEXT DEFAULT 'standard',\n    target_sets INTEGER,\n    target_reps INTEGER,\n    target_duration_seconds INTEGER,\n    target_rpe REAL,\n    target_weight REAL,\n    superset_group INTEGER,\n    notes TEXT,\n    rest_seconds INTEGER\n);\n```\n\n**Replace `sets` table** — actual performed sets (linked to planned exercises):\n```sql\nCREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS sets (\n    id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,\n    workout_exercise_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES workout_exercises(id),\n    set_number INTEGER NOT NULL,\n    reps INTEGER,\n    duration_seconds INTEGER,\n    weight REAL,\n    rpe REAL,\n    notes TEXT,\n    logged_at TEXT DEFAULT (datetime('now')),\n    UNIQUE(workout_exercise_id, set_number)\n);\n```\n\n**Relationship:** `workouts` 1→N `workout_exercises` 1→N `sets`, with `exercises` as the canonical name catalog.\n\n### Migration\nThe existing `sets` table is empty (fitness.db is unused), so we can safely drop and recreate. # Phase 1: Draft the Training Program Rulebook

## Context

Before building the workout logging system, we need the training program that Argus will use to generate workouts. This is a rulebook — not a fixed calendar — that Argus reads to dynamically create sessions based on what equipment is available, what was trained recently, and how the user is progressing.

## What to Create

**File:** `personal/health/program.md` in the Obsidian vault on the VPS (`/root/codex/personal/health/program.md`)

This file is the Argus fitness domain contract. It tells the worker model everything it needs to generate appropriate workouts.

## Structure of program.md

### 1. Program Overview
- Three pillars: Strength (calisthenics), Cardio (running), Flexibility (yoga)
- Flexible schedule — rotation-based, not day-locked
- Full body 3x/week for strength, 3x/week running, daily mobility + 2x/week dedicated yoga
- Training philosophy: gradual, structured, never to failure (leave 1-2 RIR)

### 2. Current Working Levels
- All baseline numbers with dates
- Updated as progress is made (Argus updates this after periodic tests)
- This is how Argus knows what to prescribe

### 3. Exercise Progression Ladders
For each movement pattern, the full ladder from easy to hard with the "3x8 rule" (when you hit 3x8 clean, move to next variation, drop to 3x5):

- **Push horizontal**: wall → incline → standard → diamond → decline → archer
- **Push vertical**: pike → elevated pike → wall HSPU (partial) → wall HSPU (full)
- **Push dips**: bench dips → assisted parallel → parallel → ring support → ring dips
- **Pull vertical**: dead hang → scapular pulls → negatives → band-assisted → full pull-ups → L-sit pull-ups → weighted
- **Pull horizontal**: inverted rows (high angle) → rows (low) → rows (feet elevated)
- **Squat**: air squats → split squats → Bulgarian → shrimp → pistol progressions
- **Hinge**: glute bridges → single-leg bridges → Nordic negatives → Nordic curls
- **Core**: dead bug → plank → hollow hold → hanging knee raise → L-sit (tucked → one leg → full) → hanging leg raise

Each exercise has: current variation, current working sets/reps, target to progress

### 4. Session Templates
- **Strength session structure**: warm-up (10-15 min incl. Surya Namaskar + wrist prep) → skill work (handstand, L-sit) → 3 paired exercises (push+pull supersets) → legs → core → cool-down
- **Volume/intensity**: 3-5 sets of 5-8 reps for strength, 3-4 sets of 8-12 for hypertrophy, holds at 60-70% max
- **Rest**: 90-120s between superset pairs
- **Equipment variants**: what to do at home vs park vs gym for each exercise

### 5. Running Program
**Phase 1 (current)**: Build base — 3 easy runs/week, all conversational pace, build to continuous 5K
**Phase 2 (after 4-6 weeks of base)**: Introduce speed — 1 easy + 1 interval + 1 tempo + 1 long run
**Phase 3 (after sub-28)**: Sub-23 push — 4-5 runs/week, 25-35km weekly, race-pace work
- 80/20 rule: 80% easy, 20% hard
- Paces by phase and heart rate zones
- Never do hard running + hard calisthenics same day

### 6. Yoga / Flexibility Program
- **Daily**: 15 min post-workout mobility routine (specific poses listed)
- **2x/week**: 30-45 min dedicated yin yoga sessions on rest days
- **Warm-up**: 3-5 rounds Surya Namaskar before calisthenics
- **Priority areas**: hamstrings, left shoulder, wrists, hips, t-spine
- **Pranayama**: Nadi Shodhana (daily), Sheetali (after hard sessions)

### 7. GTG Protocol
- Pull-ups: when at home, do singles/doubles spread through day (50% of max, 5-8x daily)
- Rules: never to failure, every rep crisp, spread 1+ hours apart
- Retest max every 2-3 weeks, adjust working reps
- Can be flagged when asking for a workout: "I'm home today" → includes GTG

### 8. Recovery Rules (for Argus to enforce)
- Min 48 hours between hard sessions for same muscle group
- If trained 3+ consecutive days → suggest rest day
- If 3+ days since last workout → send nudge
- Deload every 8-10 weeks (cut volume 40-60%, keep same exercises at easier variations)
- Signs to auto-suggest rest: user reports poor sleep, joint pain, or motivation drop

### 9. Nutrition Guidelines
- Small deficit: 200-300 cal below maintenance (~2000-2200 kcal/day)
- Protein: 2.0-2.4g/kg (150-180g/day)
- Fat: 0.8-1.0g/kg (60-75g/day)
- Carbs: fill remaining
- Hydrate well, especially for Pitta constitution

### 10. Milestones & Testing
- Retest baseline exercises every 4-6 weeks
- Track: max pull-ups, max push-ups, plank hold, 5K time, weight, body fat
- Milestone targets at 3, 6, 12 months (from research)

## Implementation

1. Write `program.md` to vault on VPS via SSH
2. Update `memory/project_fitness.md` with program status
3. This file will later be read by the fitness domain contract when Argus generates workouts

## Verification

- Read the file back to verify it's well-structured
- Check it covers everything needed for Argus to generate a workout with just: "I'm at the park, got 45 minutes"
